# FINDINGS: Bolmo 1B generation profile

Final runs: `results/20261026-122727` and `results/20261026-123233` (full, 25 generations each), `results/20261026-123415` (trace). Earlier smoke runs are in `results/smoke/` (the first one has an invalid step module and should be ignored).

## 1. Environment

- GPU: RTX 3060 Ti 8 GB (single GPU visible, so `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` was not set). Driver 621.01, CUDA UMD 13.5, WDDM.
- Python 3.11.9, torch 2.6.0+cu124, transformers 4.57.3 (as in the model card).
- Model `allenai/Bolmo-1B`, revision `452690a8cfab1dd1ec27b74f2667632f17a21b46` (pinned in the config), bf16, greedy, seed 1234, default prompts.
- Deviations / additions: `xlstm==2.0.6`, `mlstm_kernels==2.0.6` and `triton-windows==3.2.0.post21` were installed and pinned in `requirements.txt`. The remote code selects Triton mLSTM kernels whenever CUDA is available, and `triton-windows` made them work, so no fallback was needed.
- Ollama was stopped first. Opening it with `ollama ps` launched the app, so I killed it again. Chrome, VS Code and the Claude app were still open and holding about 1.3 GB VRAM.
- Code fix outside the measurement logic: `byteprof/runner.py` had an f-string with a backslash, which is a SyntaxError on Python 3.11. It now uses `re.escape`.

## 2. Decode table (run 2, `20261026-123233`; run 1 in brackets)

| component | calls | ms/gen. byte | % of decode |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| global_model (16 layers) | 42880 | 6.309 (6.160) | 42.4% (42.5%) |
| local_decoder (norm, proj, 4 layers) | 54420 | 5.661 (5.488) | 38.0% (37.9%) |
| local_encoder (2 embeddings, 1 layer, norm, proj) | 45350 | 1.789 (1.733) | 12.0% (12.0%) |
| lm_head | 9070 | 0.073 (0.072) | 0.5% |
| unattributed inside step | - | 1.061 (1.032) | 7.1% |
| **decode total** | 9070 steps | **14.894 (14.486)** | |

- Run-to-run difference in decode ms/byte is 2.8%, under the 5% threshold, so the machine was not noisy. SM clocks sampled between runs varied (1080-1425 MHz) but did not matter for this comparison.
- Throughput without hooks: 66.6 bytes/s (run 2) and 67.3 bytes/s (run 1). Hook overhead on wall time was +7.3% and +5.5%, so absolute ms figures are slightly inflated.
- Prefill is small: about 35 ms per generation (25 steps, 875 ms). Time outside step forwards (the generate loop, host work and lm_head bookkeeping) is about 10 s of 146 s, roughly 7%.

## 3. Bytes per patch, steps per byte

- Decode steps per generated byte: 1.000. `local_decoder` runs once per byte (54420 calls = 9070 × 6 modules).
- `global_model` calls: 42880 = 16 layers × 2680 invocations, so **3.38 bytes per patch**. This is below the expected ~4.
- Each generation produces about prompt_len + `max_new_bytes` bytes (363 on average at the default 256). Bolmo's `generate()` applies the length criterion to a list that does not contain the prompt, so `max_new_tokens` is not a true cap on new bytes. I kept this behaviour so runs stay comparable. In `generate_once` the output is sliced by `input_ids.shape[-1]` although the output holds prompt_len-1 prompt bytes, so one prompt byte is probably counted per run. This is negligible.
- Sample outputs look sensible (fluent English prose; valid-looking Python).

## 4. Kernels

`kernels.txt` is an aten-op table and `trace.json` contains CPU ops only: the torch profiler returned **no CUDA kernel events** (probably CUPTI on Windows/WDDM). I therefore cannot report top kernels by GPU time or true launch counts. The CUDA columns in `kernels.txt` look unreliable (self CUDA total 11.9 s for one 2.5 s generation) and should not be used.

What the op counts do show, for the traced `prose_en` generation of about 148 bytes:
- Heavy compute/elementwise ops sum to roughly 174k calls (mm 23.7k, copy_ 36.6k, mul 37.2k, add 20.8k, mean/rsqrt 11.5k each, pow 9.7k, cat 5.5k, addmm 4.5k, tanh 3.7k and others). That is **about 1,200 small ops, hence at least that many launches, per generated byte**. The real figure is probably higher because fills and other ops are not counted.
- Per-step time of 14.9 ms divided by about 1,200 ops is about 12 µs per op, in the range where launch and Python overhead dominate.
- Cross-check against memory bandwidth (RTX 3060 Ti about 448 GB/s): the global layers read about 1.07 GB of bf16 weights per patch (about 2.4 ms), but cost about 20.9 ms per patch measured. The local decoder reads about 0.27 GB per byte (about 0.6 ms) against 5.7 ms measured. All components run roughly 8-10× above the bandwidth floor.

## 5. What the data supports

No row of the Goal table is clean:

- Single biggest component: global_model at 42.4%. Local encoder + decoder together are 50.0%. Row 1 and row 3 are therefore close. Row 1 (fused per-byte decode kernel) is slightly ahead on combined share, and it also covers the part that runs on every byte.
- lm_head is only 0.5%, so row 4 (output-head fusion, UTF-8 masking) is not supported.
- Unattributed in-step time is only 7.1%, so row 2's "high unattributed" signal is not met in the strict sense. But the order-of-magnitude gap to the bandwidth floor and the ~1,200 tiny ops per byte point to **launch overhead inside every component**. Fusion or CUDA graphs would help all three major components and is the most defensible next step. Reducing global-model work via larger patches or drafting only attacks the 42%, and a patch averages just 3.38 bytes.
- The launch-overhead conclusion rests on op counts and the bandwidth estimate, not on a measured GPU-idle fraction, because the trace has no CUDA events.

## 6. Problems and uncertainties

- **Residual warning (acceptance criterion not fully met):** every run reports "component calls happened outside the step module (boundary_predictor, local_encoder)". The prefill boundary-prediction pass (`prefill_boundary_prediction_forward`) runs the local encoder before `model.forward`, outside any step. These calls are about 80 ms over 25 generations and do not affect decode numbers, but the warning cannot be removed by config alone.
- **Hook bypasses:** `generate()` calls `self.forward(...)` and `BolmoModel` calls `self.local_decoder.forward(...)` directly. Forward hooks never fire on those modules, so the default `step_module = ""` records no steps and a `local_decoder` match records nothing. I used `step_module = "model"` and matched the decoder's children instead. The local encoder is also matched per child because `boundary_predictor_module` is nested in it. Pooling and depooling are methods, so their time falls into "unattributed".
- **Possible bias in `timing.py` (not changed, no test added):** `lm_head` runs after the step module returns, but `HookTimer` still assigns it to the latest step. Its time is counted as attributed while not being part of step time, so "unattributed" is understated by about 0.07 ms/byte (about 0.5% of decode). Decode totals are unaffected. I did not touch the measurement code, per the rules. If you want it fixed, a failing test should come first.
- Triton on Windows worked, but it is an unofficial build (`triton-windows`).
- Hooked numbers include +5-7% overhead, and background apps share the GPU.

## Correction

Written after `TASK_byte_count.md` Part 1, before any code was changed. Throwaway probe, Bolmo 1B at the pinned revision, `max_new_tokens = 32`, greedy, all five default prompts.

| prompt | prompt bytes | input_ids | output | aligned | new bytes | step-module forwards | global forwards (layer 0) |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| prose_en | 118 | (1,119) | (1,267) | yes | 148 | 149 | 30 |
| code_py | 114 | (1,115) | (1,259) | yes | 144 | 145 | 44 |
| prose_de | 118 | (1,119) | (1,267) | yes | 148 | 149 | 31 |
| json_struct | 81 | (1,82) | (1,193) | yes | 111 | 112 | 48 |
| unicode_mix | 113 | (1,114) | (1,257) | yes | 143 | 144 | 56 |

- **(a) Unit of `max_new_tokens`:** bytes in the sense that boundary symbols are excluded, but the cap is wrong. The remote `generate()` applies the length criterion to a list of generated atoms that already starts with the last prompt byte, against `max_length = (input_len - 1) + max_new_tokens`. The effective cap on new bytes is therefore about `input_len + max_new_tokens - 3` (119 + 29 = 148, 115 + 29 = 144, 82 + 29 = 111, 114 + 29 = 143: exact for all five). At `max_new_bytes = 256` the earlier runs produced about 363 bytes per run.
- **(b) Alignment:** yes. `out[0, :input_len] == input_ids` holds for every prompt, and the decoded full output starts with the prompt text. `input_ids` has one more element than the prompt has bytes (BOS). Slicing off `input_ids.shape[-1]` removes exactly the prompt, so the harness did **not** count the prompt as generated output. The "new" text is a real continuation (e.g. " books were expensive and rare. ...").
- **(c) Byte-wise prefill:** no. The step module sees one forward with the whole prompt, shape (1, prompt_bytes), then one forward of shape (1, 1) per generated byte. Steps = 1 + new bytes. "Step 0 = prefill, the rest = decode" is correct, and decode steps per generated byte = 1.000 is genuine, not an artifact.

**Consequence:** the premise of the task (the harness counts the prompt) is not what happened. The byte counts were correct, but the generations were about 2.4 times longer than the configured cap, because Bolmo's `generate()` does not cap new bytes as documented. Per-byte numbers were measured at longer contexts (prompt + ~256 bytes) than intended. The fix is to enforce the cap through a stopping criterion; the corrected numbers are added below once the runs are done.

### Fix

- `generate_once` was not counting the prompt. The real defect was Bolmo's length cap, so the harness now passes a stopping criterion that enforces exactly `max_new_bytes` new bytes (`byteprof/counting.py`, `runner.py`). For standard HF models `max_new_bytes` is `max_new_tokens`. The output must start with the prompt ids and the decoded prompt, or `AlignmentError` is raised.
- `profile` logs an error and adds a report warning if a run exceeds its cap (Bolmo: `max_new_bytes + 3`; token models: tokens × longest vocab entry in bytes). `inspect` implements the same check and exits 2 on failure. Both configs pass `inspect` with exit 0: Bolmo produces exactly 256 new bytes and 257 step calls per prompt (1 prefill + 256 decode); OLMo 2 produces 388/288/292/172/153 bytes with 72 step calls.
- Tests: `tests/test_generate_once.py` (fake models, CPU only). The Bolmo test failed before the fix and passes after. No change to the prefill rule was needed.

### Corrected numbers (same machine, 25 generations per run, hooks on for the breakdown)

OLMo 2 1B (`allenai/OLMo-2-0425-1B`, revision `a1847dff35000b4271fa70afc5db10fd29fedbdf`) is configured with `max_new_bytes = 72` tokens, which gives a mean of 258.6 bytes per run against Bolmo's 256 (64 tokens gave 227.6). Each profile covers 6400 Bolmo bytes and 6465 OLMo bytes.

| | Bolmo 1B | OLMo 2 1B |
|---|---:|---:|
| Runs used | `20261026-132732`, `-133125` | `20261026-132033`, `-132217` |
| decode ms per generated byte | 15.92 / 15.68 (mean 15.80) | 7.19 / 6.87 (mean 7.03) |
| difference between runs | 1.5% | 4.5% |
| bytes/s without hooks | 61.7 / 63.2 | 143.0 / 145.9 |
| hook overhead on wall time | +6.5% / +7.4% | +6.5% / +3.9% |
| decode steps per generated byte | 1.000 | 0.275 (3.64 bytes per token) |
| ms per decode step | 15.8 | 25.6 |

Component shares of decode time (mean of the two runs):

| Bolmo 1B | | OLMo 2 1B | |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| global_model (16 layers) | 42.9% | global_model (16 layers) | 90.4% |
| local_decoder | 37.9% | lm_head | 4.0% |
| local_encoder | 11.6% | final_norm | 0.7% |
| lm_head | 0.5% | embed | 0.3% |
| unattributed | about 7.1% | unattributed | about 4.6% |

Bolmo decode ms per generated byte by component: global 6.78, local_decoder 6.00, local_encoder 1.84, lm_head 0.08. Global model invocations: 1920 per 6400 bytes, so **3.33 bytes per patch**.

**Ratio Bolmo : OLMo 2 decode ms per generated byte = 2.25** (15.80 / 7.03; 2.32 by no-hook throughput). On this machine the byte model is more than twice as slow per generated byte as its token source model.

Runs that were discarded or kept for the record:
- The first Bolmo pair (`20261026-131151`, `-131612`: 17.89 and 16.90 ms/byte) differed by 5.9%, over the 5% limit. GPU SM clocks drifted between about 960 and 1400 MHz and I have no permission to lock clocks, so I reran twice and used the closer pair above. The shares were the same in all four Bolmo runs (global 42.9-43.1%).
- The 17.89/16.90 pair is still under `results/`. Do not mix it into comparisons.

### What the per-component comparison adds

- A Bolmo global layer costs 1.40-1.42 ms per call and an OLMo layer 1.41-1.48 ms. The identical `BolmoDecoderLayer` and `Olmo2DecoderLayer` take the same time per call. A 67M-parameter bf16 layer should need about 0.3 ms at the memory-bandwidth floor, so both are launch-bound. Bolmo's extra cost per byte comes from running the local encoder and a 4-layer local decoder on **every byte** (about 7.8 ms per byte) while the 16 global layers run only once per 3.33 bytes (about 6.8 ms per byte) .
- OLMo 2 runs its 16 layers once per token (every 3.64 bytes), so it pays 22.6 ms per token step, which is 6.2 ms per byte. Bolmo pays about the same 6.8 ms per byte in global layers, because it generates fewer bytes per patch (3.33).
- The traces contain no CUDA events for either model on Windows (CPU ops only), so there are still no kernel timings. CPU-op events per generated byte: Bolmo about 1,990, OLMo about 600 (the first prompt of each).

### Which earlier conclusions hold

- Holds: shares are unchanged (global 42.4 -> 42.9%, local decoder 38.0 -> 37.9%, encoder 12.0 -> 11.6%, lm_head 0.5%), bytes per patch (3.38 -> 3.33), one decode step per generated byte, and the launch-overhead reading (components 8-10 times above the bandwidth floor; identical per-layer cost for both models).
- Changed: the absolute per-byte numbers. The old runs generated about 363 bytes per run and decoded at longer contexts; the old 14.5-14.9 ms/byte and 67 bytes/s were measured under those conditions. The new ones are 15.8 ms/byte and about 62 bytes/s, partly because of run-to-run clock drift on this machine. Do not compare the old absolute numbers with the new ones.
- The "premise" that the harness counted the prompt as output was wrong. The byte count itself was correct.
- Still open: the one residual warning (prefill boundary-prediction pass runs the local encoder outside the step module) and the `lm_head` attribution bias in `timing.py`.

## Overhead removal

Task: `TASK_cuda_graphs.md`. Machine: RTX 3060 Ti, bf16, Windows. Decode-loop analysis: `notes/bolmo_decode_loop.md`.

### Pre-registered hypotheses (copied verbatim before any Stage 4 measurement)

- **H1:** Optimizing only Bolmo's per-byte local loop (global model still eager)
  reduces Bolmo's decode ms per generated byte by at least 30%.
- **H2:** With only Bolmo's local loop optimized, the ratio of Bolmo decode
  ms/byte to eager OLMo 2 decode ms/byte falls below 1.5× (currently 2.25×).
- **H3:** With both models fully optimized (Bolmo: local loop + global model;
  OLMo 2: compiled forward with static KV cache), the ratio stays below 1.5×.
  (Only testable if Stage 3b succeeds.)
- **H4:** CUDA-graph replay produces byte-identical greedy output to eager mode
  for every prompt. `torch.compile` may change numerics, so for compiled
  variants report the first divergence position per prompt instead.

### Verdicts

All figures: RTX 3060 Ti, bf16, greedy, seed 1234, default prompts, Ollama not running, two full profiles per variant (`stage4.log`, `run_stage4.sh`). **Metric: no-hook wall time / new bytes, including prefill, averaged over the two runs.** Optimized runs cannot be hooked, so a hooked "decode ms/byte" exists only for eager; to compare like with like, all variants use wall/byte. Eager hooked decode ms/byte is 14.93 (Bolmo) and 6.61 (OLMo) against 15.005 and 6.357 wall/byte, so the choice of metric does not change any verdict below.

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Numbers |
|---|---|---|
| **H1** local loop only, >=30% fewer ms/byte | **Supported** | Bolmo eager 15.005 -> manual CUDA graph 8.760 ms/byte (-41.6%); `torch.compile` 8.654 (-42.3%) |
| **H2** local loop only, Bolmo/OLMo-eager ratio < 1.5x | **Supported** | 8.760 / 6.357 = **1.38x** (compile: 1.36x). Eager ratio in this session: 15.005 / 6.357 = 2.36x |
| **H3** both fully optimized, ratio < 1.5x | **Refuted, narrowly** | Bolmo local+global graphs 4.854 / OLMo compiled 3.185 = **1.52x** (per run pair 1.51x and 1.54x) |
| **H4** CUDA-graph replay byte-identical to eager | **Supported** for both graph variants; `torch.compile` diverges as the hypothesis allows | 50 of 50 measured generations per graph variant identical (5 prompts x 5 reps x 2 runs) |

Noise check (wall/byte, run 1 vs run 2): OLMo eager 2.8%, OLMo compile 4.5%, Bolmo eager 1.4%, Bolmo graph 1.5%, Bolmo graph+global 2.5%, Bolmo compile 0.3%. All under 5%, no reruns.

### Table (mean of two full runs)

| Model | Variant | ms / generated byte | bytes/s | Speedup vs own eager | Ratio to OLMo eager | Ratio to OLMo compiled |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| OLMo 2 1B | `none` | 6.357 | 157 | 1.00x | 1.00 | 2.00 |
| OLMo 2 1B | `compile` (static cache, inductor, reduce-overhead) | 3.185 | 314 | 2.00x | 0.50 | 1.00 |
| Bolmo 1B | `none` | 15.005 | 67 | 1.00x | 2.36 | 4.71 |
| Bolmo 1B | local loop, manual CUDA graph (`cuda_graph`) | 8.760 | 114 | 1.71x | 1.38 | 2.75 |
| Bolmo 1B | local loop, `torch.compile` (`compile`) | 8.654 | 116 | 1.73x | 1.36 | 2.72 |
| Bolmo 1B | local loop + global model graphs (`cuda_graph`, `optimize_global`) | 4.854 | 206 | 3.09x | 0.76 | 1.52 |

OLMo compiled runs produce ~6% fewer bytes in total (6105 vs 6465) because the outputs diverge numerically; ms/byte normalizes for that. Results: `results/20261026-160900`, `-162016` (OLMo eager), `results/olmo2-compile/*`, `results/20261026-161334`, `-162449` (Bolmo eager), `results/bolmo-graph/*`, `results/bolmo-graph-global/*`, `results/bolmo-compile/*`. Trace runs are the latest directory of each (`--runs 1 --trace`). Configs: `configs/olmo2-1b-compile.toml`, `bolmo-1b-graph.toml`, `bolmo-1b-graph-global.toml`, `bolmo-1b-compile.toml`; the eager configs are unchanged. Early development smoke runs are in `results/dev-smoke/` and are not part of the evidence.

### Correctness (first divergence vs eager, byte index; `-` = identical in every measured run)

| Variant | prose_en | code_py | prose_de | json_struct | unicode_mix |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| OLMo 2 `compile` | 112 | 136 | 125 | - | - |
| Bolmo local-loop `cuda_graph` | - | - | - | - | - |
| Bolmo local+global `cuda_graph` | - | - | - | - | - |
| Bolmo local-loop `compile` | - | 24 | 101 | - | - |

The graph variants run the same ops on the same dtypes as eager for the local layers (they reuse `xlstm._original_forward` and the same in-place state copy). The global layers use a static cache plus a boolean mask instead of an exact-length cache, which could in principle change SDPA's reduction order; on these prompts it did not. That is evidence from 5 prompts of at most 256 bytes, not a proof. Divergences of the compiled variants are bf16 numerics from fused inductor kernels; I did not check whether the diverged continuations are worse.

### What could not be captured, and why

- **The `generate()` loop itself** (`torch.tensor` of the last byte, `expand_byte_ids` on `generated.tolist()`, two `MaskState`s with `.cpu()`, `next_tokens.cpu()`, `torch.cat`, stopping criteria, `finished.all()`): about 6 host syncs per byte (counted from the source, see `notes/bolmo_decode_loop.md`), pure Python/host work. Not graphable. It is part of the remaining 4.85 ms/byte.
- **Prefill**: different code path (chunkwise kernels, variable length), left eager in every variant. Its cost is included in all wall/byte figures.
- **Boundary branch**: kept in Python (a host bool). On non-boundary steps the global graph is simply not replayed. On boundary steps, `initial_norm` plus the `last_value` copy before the decoder graph, and `model.norm` / `lm_head` after it, still run eagerly.
- **Scope limits**: batch size 1, decode steps only; other shapes raise `OptimizeError` instead of silently falling back. The global static cache holds 512 patches (OLMo: 1024 tokens); longer generations need a larger cache.
- `torch.profiler` still has no CUDA events on Windows, so the traces give CPU-side op counts only and no GPU-idle fraction. In the traced `prose_en` generation (256 bytes) the number of `aten::linear` calls visible to Python is 19,572 eager (76 per byte), 6,260 with the local loop graphed (24 per byte) and 436 with the global model graphed too (1.7 per byte). Graph replays are invisible to the profiler, so this counts removed Python-dispatched ops, not GPU kernels.

### Compile / capture time and memory

- Manual capture (3 warmups + capture): 0.4 s (local loop), 0.6-0.7 s (with global). Extra GPU memory (allocated, after warmup, vs eager): 19 MiB for the local loop (fp32 mLSTM state of 5 layers) and 88 MiB with the global graph (static KV cache, 512 patches, 16 layers, bf16).
- `torch.compile` of the Bolmo local loop: 4.3 s in `apply()`, +3 MiB. OLMo compile: first warmup pass 11.5 s vs 3.9 s steady state, so about 7.6 s of compile, +128 MiB (static cache, 1024 tokens). Caveat: the inductor on-disk cache was already warm from my development runs, so a cold compile will take longer. Compile and capture happen in warmup and are not in the measured wall times.
- Peak allocated memory is about 2.9-3.0 GiB for all variants; the 8 GB card is not a constraint.

### What this means for the byte-specific optimization ideas

Launch and Python overhead was the dominant cost. Graphing only the five local layers removes 41% of Bolmo's time per byte, and adding the global model brings Bolmo to 4.85 ms/byte: 3.1x faster than eager and 24% faster than eager OLMo 2, though still 1.52x slower than compiled OLMo 2 (3.19 ms/byte). The goal table's "launch overhead -> fusion / CUDA graphs" row is therefore confirmed by an intervention, not just by op counts. The remaining gap to compiled OLMo 2 is 1.67 ms/byte. I have no hooked breakdown of the remaining 4.85 ms, so I cannot say how much is the `generate()` host loop (about 6 syncs and a Python trie walk over the growing byte list per byte, none of it model compute), how much is the eager boundary-step prelude, and how much is real mLSTM/MLP compute. A fused per-byte decode kernel can at best remove part of that 1.67 ms/byte, and the gap contains host work no kernel touches, so I would not build the kernel next. A cheaper next step is to remove the host loop's per-byte work (subword ids from a precomputed table, no `MaskState` syncs, batched `.cpu()`) and re-measure; only if the graphed local loop still dominates after that does a custom kernel look justified. The lookup-table drafter idea attacks global-model/patch cost, which is now graphed and no longer launch-bound, so its benefit should be re-estimated from this profile rather than the eager one. These conclusions hold for batch 1 on one Windows consumer GPU; the Colab notebook (`VARIANTS`) can repeat them on Linux, which I have not run.



## Minimal-sync loop

Task: `TASK_minimal_sync_loop.md`. Builds on `## Overhead removal` above (graph-global Bolmo 4.85 ms/byte vs compiled OLMo 2 3.19, 1.52x).

### Pre-registered hypotheses (copied verbatim before any Stage 0 or Stage 3 measurement)

- **H5:** In the existing graph-global variant, time spent outside graph
  replays and global calls (host loop) is at least 40% of its 4.85 ms/byte.
- **H6:** With the current `generate()` loop, graph-global Bolmo's ms/byte
  grows with generation length (64 → 1024 new bytes) by at least 20%, because
  of the O(n) history walk. With the minimal loop it changes by less than 5%.
- **H7:** The minimal-sync loop brings graph-global Bolmo below 3.5 ms/byte at
  256 new bytes.
- **H8:** Minimal-sync Bolmo vs. compiled OLMo 2 (HF `generate`): ratio below
  1.25x.
- **H9:** With both models on equivalent minimal loops (Stage 3), the ratio is
  still below 1.25x.
- **H10:** The minimal loop's output is byte-identical to eager Bolmo for all
  prompts and lengths. The incremental automaton returns exactly the same
  subword ids as `tokenizer.expand_byte_ids` on the test corpus from Stage 1.

### 1. Verdicts

Same machine and conditions as `## Overhead removal` (RTX 3060 Ti, bf16, greedy, seed 1234, default prompts, Ollama not running), two runs per cell. Metric: no-hook wall time / new bytes, including prefill, unless a row says "decode". Graph-global + `hf` loop is the 4.85 ms/byte variant of the previous section; it measured 4.84 and 5.04 in this session (mean 4.94 at 256 bytes).

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Numbers |
|---|---|---|
| **H5** host loop >= 40% of 4.85 ms/byte | **Refuted** | Outside replays (host loop plus idle gaps): 1.01 ms/byte = **21%** of the decode phase (4.83 ms/byte), 24% of the full wall time if prefill (0.17) is counted as outside. 64 and 1024 bytes give 20.5% and 21.1% |
| **H6** `hf` loop grows >= 20% from 64 to 1024 bytes; minimal changes < 5% | **Refuted** | `hf` wall/byte falls 5.28 -> 4.77 (-9.8%, prefill amortization); decode phase alone 4.69 -> 4.75 (+1.3%). Minimal: wall/byte 4.41 -> 3.85 (-12.6%, also prefill amortization, so the "< 5%" half is not met on this metric); decode phase alone 3.840 (256) -> 3.839 (1024), i.e. flat, but I have no decode-only run at 64 for the minimal loop |
| **H7** minimal loop < 3.5 ms/byte at 256 | **Refuted** | 3.967 ms/byte (3.968 and 3.966). Not reachable by removing the host loop: the graph replays alone take 3.79 ms/byte on the GPU |
| **H8** minimal Bolmo / compiled OLMo (`hf`) < 1.25x | **Refuted, narrowly** | 3.967 / 3.146 = **1.26x**. OLMo `hf` = mean of 4 runs within 1.3% (3.140, 3.136, 3.174, 3.133); the first `hf` run (3.387) differed from its partner by 7.5% and was discarded per the 5% rule. Including it the ratio would be 1.23x |
| **H9** both on minimal loops, ratio < 1.25x | **Refuted** | 3.967 / 2.580 = **1.54x** (OLMo minimal: 5 runs within 2.6%) |
| **H10** minimal output byte-identical to eager; automaton identical to `expand_byte_ids` | **Supported** | Bolmo minimal: first divergence `-` for all 5 prompts in every measured run at 64, 256 and 1024 bytes (2 runs x 5 reps x 5 prompts each). State diff on `prose_en`, 128 bytes: 0 of 128 steps differ in any recurrent state (5 layers), `last_value` or subword id. Automaton: exact on ~30,000 positions of the real tokenizer (see section 4) |

Also: OLMo minimal output is identical to OLMo compiled with HF `generate()` on all 5 prompts (both diverge from eager at the same bytes, 112/136/125).

### 2. Stage 0: breakdown of the old (`hf`) loop, and ms/byte vs length

Instrumented runs (`--instrument timing`): every graph replay is bracketed by two CUDA events (GPU time, resolved after the generation, so no extra sync) and `perf_counter` (host time inside the call). "Outside replays" = decode-phase wall time minus the summed GPU time of all replays (enc, dec, glob) = host loop, eager small ops, idle gaps and sync waits. Instrumentation perturbation (timing runs vs plain runs, wall/byte): +0.7% (64), +0.8% (256), +0.5% (1024); minimal loop +0.5% (256), +0.6% (1024). No sync is added: events are only read after the generation's final sync.

ms per generated byte, mean of 2 runs (`results/minsync/s0-timing-*`):

| new bytes | prefill | enc replay | dec replay | global replay | outside replays | decode phase | wall (timing run) | wall (plain, 2 runs) |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 64 | 0.646 | 0.342 | 1.103 | 2.283 | 0.964 | 4.691 | 5.320 | 5.284 (5.266, 5.302) |
| 256 | 0.167 | 0.343 | 1.120 | 2.354 | 1.009 | 4.825 | 4.985 | 4.943 (4.841, 5.044) |
| 1024 | 0.043 | 0.342 | 1.104 | 2.306 | 1.003 | 4.753 | 4.791 | 4.765 (4.765, 4.765) |

- The global model runs on 0.30 of the bytes (3.3 bytes per patch) and costs 7.7 ms per call inside the graph; per byte it is the largest replay.
- Host syncs in the `hf` decode loop: **9.0 per byte** (`torch.cuda.set_sync_debug_mode("warn")` counted 2306 for 256 steps; 5 of them come from the harness's `.tolist()` calls after `generate()` returns). The task description estimated about 6. Python-level wrappers counted `.item()` 2251, `.cpu()` 768, `.tolist()` 259, `__bool__` 256 per generation; `.item()` also counts CPU-tensor reads inside `MaskState` that are not syncs, so only the debug-mode total is a sync count.
- Cost of the O(n) history walk: the reference `expand_byte_ids(history, n_last=1)` takes about 47 us per call at histories up to 2000 bytes (mean over 1..2000), against 1.0 ms/byte outside replays in total. The quadratic term exists but is about 1-5% of the outside time at these lengths, which is why H6 fails.
- Pair differences: plain 256 bytes 4.1%; everything else under 2%. All under 5%.

### 3. Table: model x loop x length

| Model | Loop | New bytes | ms/byte | bytes/s | Host syncs per byte | Ratio to compiled OLMo `hf` (3.146) | Ratio to compiled OLMo minimal (2.580) |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Bolmo, graph-global | `hf` | 64 | 5.284 | 189 | 9.0 | 1.68 | 2.05 |
| Bolmo, graph-global | `hf` | 256 | 4.943 | 202 | 9.0 | 1.57 | 1.92 |
| Bolmo, graph-global | `hf` | 1024 | 4.765 | 210 | 9.0 | 1.51 | 1.85 |
| Bolmo, graph-global | minimal | 64 | 4.406 | 227 | 1.0 | 1.40 | 1.71 |
| Bolmo, graph-global | minimal | 256 | **3.967** | 252 | 1.0 | **1.26** | 1.54 |
| Bolmo, graph-global | minimal | 1024 | 3.852 | 260 | 1.0 | 1.22 | 1.49 |
| OLMo 2, compiled | `hf` (HF generate) | 72 tokens (~256 bytes) | 3.146 | 318 | not measured | 1.00 | 1.22 |
| OLMo 2, compiled | minimal | 72 tokens (~256 bytes) | 2.580 | 388 | 1.0 by construction, not counted | 0.82 | 1.00 |

Speedups: Bolmo minimal vs graph-global `hf` 1.25x at 256 bytes (1.20x at 64, 1.24x at 1024); vs eager (15.0 ms/byte) 3.8x. OLMo minimal vs OLMo `hf` 1.22x. The OLMo rows are at one length only, so the length columns of the Bolmo rows are not comparable at 64 and 1024 to OLMo (ratio columns for those rows are shown for orientation).

Sync count for the minimal loop (`--instrument syncs`, 256 bytes x 5 prompts): 256 explicit `Event.synchronize()` calls per 256 decode steps = **exactly 1 per byte**, and the sync-debug mode saw no implicit sync inside the loop (its total of 5 per generation is the harness's `.tolist()` after `generate()` returned; prefill is excluded because counting starts at the first decode step).

Minimal Bolmo decode phase (timing runs, ms/byte): enc 0.347, dec 1.131, global 2.310, outside replays **0.053** (was 1.01), prefill 0.156 (256 bytes). The host loop was removed almost entirely; what is left is GPU time.

### 4. Correctness and automaton equivalence (H10)

- `byteprof/optim/subword_automaton.py`: Aho-Corasick over the 100,278 vocabulary entries (216,894 states). `step(state, byte) -> (state, subword_id)`. Semantics read from `tokenization_bolmo.py`: fused-boundary ids (>= offset + 256) lose the flag, `<bpe_token_end>` ids are skipped, identical sequences resolve to the later token, no match raises (the reference asserts). Build 3.1 s, 28 MiB after build (55 MiB peak). `step()`: 0.39 us/byte with an empty transition memo, 0.22 us/byte warm.
- Tests (`tests/test_subword_automaton.py`, CPU, synthetic vocabulary): longest-suffix match vs a clone of the reference trie walk and vs brute force on 20 random vocabularies x 300 positions (with boundary flags), duplicate sequences, `<bpe_token_end>`, multi-byte UTF-8 entries, no-match fallback. One test (`real_tokenizer`, skipped without the local download) compares against the real `expand_byte_ids` at every position of: the 5 default prompts, 5 texts Bolmo generated (about 1000 bytes each, `tests/data/bolmo_generated.json`), 12,001 random byte ids with random boundary flags, and 12,001 random valid UTF-8 ids. All equal.
- Per-byte state diff (`tools/debug_minimal_loop.py`): reference = original `generate()` with a hook on `BolmoModel` recording all 15 recurrent-state tensors, `last_value` and the subword id passed in after each step; compared with the static buffers after each byte of the minimal loop: 128 of 128 bytes identical bitwise.
- State of the original loop that the minimal loop deliberately does not reproduce, because it never affects the model: `bytes_since_boundary` (written, never read), the `MaskState` objects (at batch 1 they reduce to the host bool "previous byte had the boundary flag"), `tokens_generated_plus_prefilled`, `bytes_generated`. The stop condition is evaluated on the host from the byte already read (no extra sync) rather than on the GPU.
- Deviations from the task's wording: the decoder graph additionally contains `model.norm`, `lm_head` and argmax (same ops as eager, bit-identical), with two decoder variants (with/without new patch) so the boundary prelude is also inside a graph; a byte plus its boundary flag is one int64 token id (>= 260 means boundary), so reading the byte reads the flag too.
- Limits of the minimal loop: batch size 1, greedy, needs `optimize = "cuda_graph"` with `optimize_global`; stopping criteria are count-based only (harness `_MaxAtoms`, max length, EOS); a generated `<bpe_token_end>` raises. A fresh `generate()` call reloads the post-prefill state into the graph buffers; prefill is the original code path.

### 5. What remains, and what it points to

Of the 3.97 ms/byte, 3.79 is the GPU executing the three graphs (enc 0.35 + dec 1.13 + global 2.31), 0.16 is prefill and 0.05 is everything else. The host loop is no longer a significant cost, so there is no further gain in loop work. The gap to compiled OLMo 2 (1.26x against its HF loop, 1.54x against its minimal loop) is GPU time.

Weight-bandwidth floors from the safetensors headers (bf16, RTX 3060 Ti 448 GB/s): global layers 2.148 GB = 4.79 ms per call, local decoder 0.282 GB = 0.63 ms, local encoder layers 0.077 GB = 0.17 ms, `lm_head` negligible, embeddings are row lookups. Measured replay times are 62%, 56% and 49% of the peak-bandwidth rate. Per byte that is 1.0 GB of weights for Bolmo (0.30 patches x 2.148 + 0.282 + 0.077) against about 0.75 GB for OLMo 2 (2.55 GB per token including the 0.41 GB output head, 3.39 bytes per token). The ratio of weights read per byte is 1.33x, the measured minimal-vs-minimal ratio 1.54x: **at batch 1 both models are mostly memory-bound, and Bolmo reads more weights per output byte.** (This also corrects `## 4. Kernels` above, which counted the global layers' 1.07 G parameters as 1.07 GB; in bf16 it is 2.15 GB, so the eager figure of 20.9 ms per patch was about 4.4x, not 8-10x, above the floor.)

Which idea this points to (inference from the floors, none of it tested here):
- **Fused per-byte decode kernel:** the local encoder and decoder together take 1.48 ms/byte against a 0.80 ms floor. Even a perfect fusion would save at most about 0.6-0.7 ms/byte (17%), and realistically less. Worth doing only after bigger levers.
- **Multi-byte drafting (verify several bytes per weight read):** it attacks the actual bottleneck, because each extra drafted byte that is verified in the same pass reuses the weights just read. The local encoder and decoder weights (0.36 GB of the 1.0 GB per byte) are read once per byte today, the global model once per patch. This is the largest byte-specific lever in the data; the drafter's acceptance rate is the open question.
- **Weight precision** (int8/fp8 weights) would halve the bytes read but is not byte-specific.
- Nothing more to gain from the host loop. The remaining Bolmo/OLMo gap is explained to a good approximation by weight bytes per output byte, so it is an architectural cost of running a 1B-parameter global model plus local models per byte, not an implementation inefficiency of the loop.

### 6. Problems and uncertainties

- The first OLMo `hf` run (3.387 ms/byte) was an outlier (7.5% from its partner); discarded, three more runs added (`results/minsync/s4c-*`). The OLMo `hf` mean (3.146) decides H8 by 0.01 (1.26 vs the 1.25 line), so H8 should be read as "about 1.25x", not as a clear miss.
- I ran `pytest` for about 10 seconds during the Stage 0 timing run at 256 bytes (first of the pair, 5.022 vs 4.948); the pair is within 1.5%.
- OLMo's host-sync count was not instrumented; its minimal loop has one `Event.synchronize()` per token by construction.
- The Windows traces (`--trace`, `results/minsync/s4-*-trace`) contain no CUDA events, so the launch-gap / sync-bubble split (`byteprof/tracegaps.py`, new Colab cell) is tested only on a synthetic trace and has not been run on a real one.
- The `real_tokenizer` test needs the Bolmo tokenizer in the local HF cache and is skipped otherwise.
- All numbers are batch 1 on one Windows GPU with drifting clocks; the Colab notebook (`VARIANTS` now lists the minimal variants) has not been run.




## Local-stack quantization

Task: `TASK_local_quant.md`. Builds on `## Minimal-sync loop` (Bolmo graph-global + minimal loop 3.967 ms/byte, OLMo 2 compiled minimal 2.580, ratio 1.54x). Code: `byteprof/quant/` (selection, accounting), `tools/byte_accounting.py` (Stage 0 numbers, `results/quant/stage0.json`).

### Pre-registered hypotheses (copied verbatim before any Stage 1, 3 or 4 measurement)

- **H11 (bandwidth model):** for each quantized variant, the measured
  reduction in ms/byte is within ±30% (relative) of the reduction predicted from
  bytes saved per output byte (Stage 0).
- **H12 (local int8):** weight-only int8 on the local stack reduces minimal-loop
  Bolmo ms/byte by at least 10%.
- **H13 (local int4):** weight-only int4 on the local stack reduces it by at
  least 20%.
- **H14 (amplification):** time saved per GB of weights quantized is at least
  2x larger for local-only quantization than for global-only quantization with
  the same scheme (bandwidth model: ~3.4x).
- **H15 (quality, int8):** local int8 raises bits-per-byte by less than 1%
  (relative), and CUTE accuracy drops by less than 1 point.
- **H16 (quality, int4):** local int4 raises bits-per-byte by less than 3%
  (relative), and CUTE accuracy drops by less than 3 points.

### Stage 0: byte accounting and predictions (written before any measurement of the quantized path)

**Assumption (stated explicitly).** Per component group, GPU time per output byte is proportional to the weight bytes read; time that is not weight reading (prefill, host loop, 0.177 ms/byte) stays constant. Calibration is the measured bf16 minimal-loop decode (`## Minimal-sync loop`, section 3): local = enc 0.347 + dec 1.131 = 1.478 ms/byte, global = 2.310 ms/byte, constant = 3.967 - 3.788 = 0.179 ms/byte. Each group keeps its own bf16 efficiency (56%/49% of peak for the local graphs, 62% for global), so a quantized kernel is assumed to reach the same fraction of peak bandwidth as the bf16 one; Stage 1 tests exactly that. Everything not counted as weights (recurrent state ~40 MB read+written per byte, KV cache, activations) is inside the proportional time of its group, which makes the prediction slightly optimistic. Bytes per patch 3.38, bytes per token 3.39 (earlier measurements). Scheme sizes: int8 = 1 B/element + one bf16 scale per output row; int4 g128 = 0.5 B/element + one bf16 (scale, zero) pair per 128 inputs per output row. Selected for quantization (`byteprof/quant/select.py`): the linears of the local encoder and decoder layers (`xlstm.q/k/v/ogate_preact/out_proj`, `mlp.gate/up/down_proj`), `local_encoder.out_projection`, `local_decoder.in_projection`; global: the 7 linears of each of the 16 global layers. **Not** quantized: embeddings (one row read per step), `lm_head` (520 outputs, 2.1 MB per byte), the boundary predictor (prefill only, it decides patching), and the 16-row mLSTM `igate_preact`/`fgate_preact` projections (65 KB each; deviation from the task's "mLSTM gates" wording, deliberate: they are two of the five mLSTM projections but tiny and set the recurrence gates). Biases and norms stay bf16.

Weight MB read per output byte and predicted time (Bolmo 1B, `tools/byte_accounting.py`):

| variant | local enc | local dec | global | lm_head | embed | total MB | predicted ms/byte | predicted reduction |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 | 76.7 | 281.6 | 635.4 | 2.13 | 0.008 | 995.9 | 3.967 (measured) | 0.0% |
| local int8 | 38.5 | 141.2 | 635.4 | 2.13 | 0.008 | 817.2 | 3.230 | 18.6% |
| local int4 | 20.5 | 75.2 | 635.4 | 2.13 | 0.008 | 733.3 | 2.884 | 27.3% |
| global int4 | 76.7 | 281.6 | 168.8 | 2.13 | 0.008 | 529.3 | 2.271 | 42.8% |
| all int4 | 20.5 | 75.2 | 168.8 | 2.13 | 0.008 | 266.7 | 1.188 | 70.1% |

OLMo 2 1B (layers 633.6 MB, `lm_head` 121.3 MB per output byte, never quantized): bf16 754.8 MB, all-layers int4 289.6 MB; predicted 2.580 -> 1.100 ms/byte (-57.4%), with OLMo's non-weight time assumed to be the same 0.179 ms/byte as Bolmo's. Predicted Bolmo/OLMo ratio at int4 (all layers): 1.188 / 1.100 = 1.08x, down from 1.54x.

Predicted ms saved per GB of bf16 weights quantized (H14), local 0.358 GB and global 2.147 GB of stored bf16 weights: int8 local 2.06 ms/GB vs global 0.54 ms/GB (**3.83x**); int4 local 3.03 vs 0.79 (**3.84x**). It is a little above the pure frequency ratio of 3.38 because the global graph runs at a higher bandwidth fraction.

Per-variant acceptance windows for H11 (predicted reduction in ms/byte, ±30%): local int8 0.737 ms (0.516-0.958), local int4 1.083 (0.758-1.408), global int4 1.696 (1.187-2.205), all int4 2.779 (1.945-3.613). With these predictions H12 (>=10%: predicted 18.6%) and H13 (>=20%: predicted 27.3%) are expected to hold if Stage 1 shows the kernels reach bf16-like bandwidth; H14, H15 and H16 are not predicted by the byte model (H15/H16 are quality hypotheses).


### Environment and deviations

RTX 3060 Ti, driver 621.01, torch 2.6.0+cu124, transformers 4.57.3, **torchao 0.9.0** (installed with `--no-deps`), pyarrow 26.0.0, triton-windows 3.2.0.post21; Bolmo `452690a8...`, OLMo 2 `a1847dff...`. Datasets: WikiText-2 raw test, `Salesforce/wikitext` revision `b08601e0...` (first 200,000 bytes, 196 chunks of 1024 bytes); CUTE `leukas/cute` revision `34e3d23f...` (500 items, 14 task types, 36/35 per type, seed 20261026). Ollama was not running. **Chrome was open** (idle, no video) and could not be closed by me; GPU utilization before runs was 4-11%. Deviations from the task wording:
- **int8 does not use torchao.** torchao 0.9's int8 weight-only path (eager and compiled) is slower than bf16 (Stage 1), and torch 2.6 has no CUDA `_weight_int8pack_mm`. The int8 variant uses a small fused Triton GEMV I wrote (`byteprof/quant/triton_int8.py`, per-row symmetric int8, dequantized in registers, bf16 scales). int4 is torchao `int4_weight_only(group_size=128)` (tinygemm). So H12 tests "int8 with a hand-written fused kernel", not torchao.
- `igate_preact` / `fgate_preact` (16 output rows) are not quantized (see Stage 0).
- Int4 needed a guard for zero-row inputs (Bolmo applies `out_projection` to an empty tensor on non-boundary steps; the kernel fails with "invalid configuration argument"). The guard returns an empty tensor, as a bf16 linear does.
- Int8 prefill (more than one row) dequantizes the same int8 weights to bf16 per call. The speed runs use the fused kernel for every decode step; bits-per-byte is mostly prefill-style, so it measures the same quantized weights with a different (more exact) matmul. CUTE generation uses the decode kernels.
- **bits per byte definition.** Bolmo's `lm_head` predicts fused (byte, patch-boundary) tokens. `bpb` = teacher-forced NLL of the fused tokens / bytes, boundaries from the model's own boundary predictor over each 1024-byte chunk (a chain-rule probability of the fused sequence, so no leakage, but it also pays for the flags: an upper bound on the byte NLL). `marginal bpb` sums p(b,0)+p(b,1) per position (the context still contains the model's flags). The two move together in every variant. These are not comparable to bpb numbers of other models. Chunks start at BOS with no context from the previous chunk.
- CUTE prompt: the dataset's 4-shot prompt + ` Answer: "`, greedy, answer = text up to the next quote, stripped, exact match; generation budget = gold answer bytes + 8 (same for all variants). Absolute accuracy is not comparable to the paper.
- The first-divergence column compares against the bf16 minimal-loop output of the same session (`outputs.json` of the first bf16 run). The "reference" check inside each run compares against the same variant's eager output and was identical for all variants (smoke runs: `divergence=None`).

### Stage 1: kernel table (batch 1, inside a CUDA graph, ring of 8 distinct weights, median of 5, `tools/quant_kernel_bench.py`)

| shape (out x in) | calls per layer | bf16 us (GB/s) | torchao int8 eager us | **Triton int8** us (GB/s) | **torchao int4 g128** us (GB/s) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1024 x 2048 (q, k) | 2 | 14.7 (286) | 38.9 | 8.4 (250) | 7.6 (147) |
| 2048 x 2048 (v, ogate, out_proj, in/out_projection) | 3 | 24.5 (342) | 75.7 | 14.2 (296) | 10.5 (211) |
| 2816 x 2048 (mlp gate, up) | 2 | 35.7 (323) | 105.7 | 18.4 (315) | 12.6 (243) |
| 2048 x 2816 (mlp down) | 1 | 39.3 (294) | 107.9 | 18.7 (309) | 13.9 (221) |
| **one layer's 8 linears** | | 213.6 | 624 (**0.34x**) | 114.9 (**1.86x**) | 85.8 (**2.49x**) |

GB/s = stored weight bytes / time. Compiled torchao (`torch.compile` per module, `stage1-compiled.json`): int8 17.9 / 111 / 51.7 / 99.5 us (noisy, min-max up to 30%, never reliably below bf16), int4 18.6 / 28.6 / 32.2 / 23.9 us (2-3x **slower** than eager int4). `torch._weight_int8pack_mm` has no CUDA kernel in torch 2.6. The fused kernels read fewer bytes but reach a lower bandwidth fraction than bf16 (int4 147-243 GB/s vs 286-342), so the layer speedup (2.49x for int4, 1.86x for int8) is below the byte ratio (about 3.5x and 2x). **Checkpoint 1 passed**: int4 2.49x >= 2x and fused int8 1.86x >= 1.4x. The lever exists on this stack for int4 (torchao) and int8 (own kernel), not for torchao's int8.

### Checkpoint 2

All four Bolmo variants generate coherent text, capture into the graph-global + minimal-loop variant, and keep one `Event.synchronize` per byte (64 calls for 64 bytes in the sync-instrumented smoke runs; the 5 implicit syncs per generation are the harness's `.tolist()` after `generate()`). Output within each run equals that variant's own eager output (`divergence=None`).

### Report points

**3. Variant table**: see the Verdicts table above (ms/byte predicted vs measured, weight bytes per output byte, bpb with CI, CUTE with CI, peak memory, first divergence). Peak memory is `max_memory_allocated` after warmup (it drops from 2932 to 1168 MiB for all-int4; the static caches and graph buffers are included). First divergence: every quantized variant leaves bf16's output within the first 1-100 bytes on most prompts (only local int8 is identical on 2 of 5 prompts), which is expected for any perturbation of the weights and says nothing about quality by itself; the bpb and CUTE columns are the quality evidence.

**4. H14**: see the verdict row. Per GB of weights quantized, the local stack saves 4.06x more time than the global model, above the pure call-frequency ratio of 3.38x. I did not isolate why (the global layers' shapes, 2048x8192, were not microbenchmarked in Stage 1, so I cannot say whether the global int4 kernels are less efficient than the local ones).

**5. OLMo 2 at equal precision** (compiled forward, minimal loop, 72 tokens ~ 256 bytes, `lm_head` and embeddings bf16): bf16 2.544 ms/byte (2.547, 2.541), all-layers int4 1.890 (1.895, 1.885). Bolmo/OLMo ratio: bf16 3.978 / 2.544 = **1.56x** (1.54x in the earlier section); all int4 1.992 / 1.890 = **1.05x**. Caveats: OLMo's int4 saving (0.654 ms, 26%) is far below its Stage 0 prediction (1.48 ms, 57%), because its forward goes through `torch.compile`, and Stage 1 showed compiled torchao int4 running 2-3x slower than eager int4 (the fused tinygemm op is not used efficiently under inductor). OLMo int4 is therefore a pessimistic measurement; a faster OLMo would push the 1.05x ratio up. I did not run OLMo quality (optional). OLMo's untouched 0.12 GB `lm_head` is 42% of its int4 bytes; Bolmo's head is 2 MB.

**6. Does the bandwidth model hold, and what follows for drafting?** Qualitatively yes, and the intervention supports the earlier arithmetic: shrinking bytes read speeds decode up in the predicted order (local int8 < local int4 < global int4 < all int4: 14%, 21%, 31%, 50%), the local weights are worth about 4x more per GB than the global ones, and Bolmo's gap to OLMo 2 closes from 1.56x to 1.05x when both are int4. Quantitatively it overpredicts every saving by 23-29% (all within the pre-registered +-30%), and Stage 1 explains where it goes: the low-bit kernels read fewer bytes but reach 40-75% of the bf16 kernels' bandwidth, so a 2x/3.5x byte reduction became a 1.86x/2.49x kernel speedup, and the local-stack speedup in the full model (int4 local 1.478 -> 0.63 ms, 2.3x; int8 1.6x) matches those kernel numbers. The model also leaves out about 0.18 ms/byte of constant time that now matters more (at 1.99 ms/byte it is 9%). For drafting: the local stack costs 1.48 ms/byte in bf16 (37% of the 3.98 ms) and is read once per byte; drafting that verifies k bytes per weight read amortizes those reads (and the global read) k-fold, so its benefit is bounded by the weight-read share, which this experiment confirms is real (about 3/4 of bf16 time scales with bytes read) but also shows the realistic ceiling of a bandwidth-saving trick on this software stack is about 70-75% of the byte-ratio prediction. Quantizing the local stack to int4 already takes 21% of the time at +1.2% bpb, so a drafter has to beat that on quality-adjusted terms; combining both is plausible since int4 makes the per-byte floor smaller and the constant per-step costs (graph launches, one sync, ~0.18 ms) the next limit. None of the drafter's acceptance rate is measured here.

### Problems and uncertainties

- Int8 results depend on my own Triton kernel and per-row (not per-group) scales; a better int8 (grouped, torchao in a newer torch) could differ in both speed and quality. Int8 and int4 use different quantization schemes, so int8 vs int4 comparisons mix kernel and scheme effects.
- The kernel microbenchmarks use random weights; real weights could change int4 error, not speed.
- CUTE with 500 items has CIs of about +-4.5 points; H16's CUTE half is only supported by the point estimate (-2.2, CI to -4.4). The CIs for bpb are over chunks and the paired change CIs are narrow because quantization shifts all chunks similarly; they do not include uncertainty from the boundary predictor changing between variants.
- bpb mixes in the cost of coding boundary flags, and quantized variants may choose different boundaries (the global/boundary bookkeeping changes with the weights). I did not separate these effects.
- Peak memory is allocator peak after warmup, not `nvidia-smi` process memory. Chrome was running throughout (see Environment). Quality and speed come from the same session and library versions; the only reruns were none.
- Torchao 0.9 on Windows runs without its C++ extensions (warnings printed); the int4 path used here is a torch op and did not need them.
- Colab notebook not updated or run.


## Fair ratio, global int8, floor

Task: `TASK_fair_ratio_floor.md`. Builds on `## Local-stack quantization` (Bolmo bf16 3.978, all int4 1.992 ms/byte; OLMo 2 bf16 2.544).

### Pre-registered hypotheses and predictions (copied verbatim before any measurement for this task)

Copy everything in this section **verbatim** into a new
`## Fair ratio, global int8, floor` section of `FINDINGS.md` **before** any
measurement for this task. Never edit it afterwards. Mark each hypothesis
supported, refuted or not tested, with numbers.

**My prediction for the fair ratio** (from the fitted model, k ≈ 2.83 ms per
GB/byte): OLMo 2's transformer blocks are the same size as Bolmo's global model
(~1.07 G params), and its untied lm_head adds ~0.21 G params, read once per
token (~3.6 bytes). With the blocks in int4 (~0.27 of bf16 bytes including
group scales) and lm_head in bf16, OLMo should land near 1.35 ms/byte, giving a
ratio near **1.45x** against Bolmo all-int4 (1.99). With OLMo's lm_head in int4
too, OLMo approaches ~1.15 ms/byte and the ratio ~1.75x. So the earlier 1.05x
is expected to be an artifact of OLMo's slow kernel path.

- **H17:** With matched kernels, loop and rule (transformer blocks int4 in both;
  embeddings and lm_head in bf16 in both), the Bolmo/OLMo 2 ratio is between
  1.3x and 1.6x.
- **H18:** With OLMo 2's lm_head also in int4 (its best case), the ratio is at
  least 1.5x.
- **H19:** Bolmo local int4 + global int8 runs at ≤ 2.6 ms/byte, with a bpb
  increase < 2% and a CUTE drop < 3 points (point estimate) vs bf16.
- **H20:** In Bolmo all-int4, at least 0.6 ms/byte of decode time is
  per-kernel overhead: GPU time in kernels shorter than 10 µs plus gaps between
  nodes inside graph replays. The sync and its memcpy account for less than
  0.3 ms/byte, and mLSTM state traffic (analytic) for less than 0.1 ms/byte.


### Environment, setup and deviations

Same machine and versions as `## Local-stack quantization` (RTX 3060 Ti, torch 2.6.0+cu124, torchao 0.9.0, triton-windows 3.2.0.post21), plus Nsight Systems 2023.4.4. All speed and quality numbers below are from one session (2026-10-26, 19:55-21:5x). Ollama not running; Chrome open and idle (cannot be closed by me). Speed: 256 new bytes (OLMo 72 tokens), 25 generations per run, two runs per variant in opposite order; all pairs agree within 1.3%, so no reruns. Code: `byteprof/quant/` (OLMo targets `blocks`, `blocks+head`; `local_scheme`/`global_scheme` overrides), `byteprof/optim/olmo.py` (`make_generate_graph`), `tools/make_fair_configs.py`, `run_fair_stage_ab.sh`, `run_fair_quality.sh`, `tools/fair_report.py`, `tools/graph_node_floor.py`, `tools/sync_cost_check.py`, `tools/run_nsys.py`, `tools/nsys_trace.py`, `tools/nsys_analyze.py`, `tools/floor_decomposition.py`; results in `results/fair/`, tables in `results/fair/report.md`.

- **Stage A0 (kernel tests).** `tests/test_quant_int8.py` now compares the Triton int8 GEMV and `Int8Linear` with an fp32 `x @ dequant(W).T` reference on the real local and global shapes (including 8192x2048 and 2048x8192), odd N and K (37x1000, 1x8, 3x129, 1025x2049, 16x2047, 7x5000) and non-default block configs, with M = 1, 2, 5, 33, 300 (with and without bias), the empty input (0 rows), and inside a CUDA graph. All 35 tests pass (tolerance 1% of the largest output element, which is about bf16 output rounding). The earlier test had already found one real flaw (scale rounded after quantizing; fixed before the last task's runs).
- **OLMo path.** The OLMo variants run in OLMo's static KV cache (1024 positions) in a **manually captured CUDA graph of the eager HF forward**, with argmax, next token, cache position, position ids and mask update inside the graph, and per token one replay, one pinned-memory read and one `Event.synchronize` (72 tokens -> 72 explicit syncs; 10 further implicit syncs per generation are prefill and the harness's `.tolist()`). No `torch.compile` is involved, and the quantized linears are the same two kernels as Bolmo's (torchao int4 g128, Triton int8), captured in the graph without problems. Output within each OLMo variant equals its own eager output. **Consequence: OLMo bf16 in this setup is 2.669 ms/byte, not the 2.544 of the compiled variant** (eager kernels in a graph are not fused by inductor), so all OLMo ratios below use this graph baseline; against the compiled bf16 number the Bolmo/OLMo bf16 ratio would be 1.57x instead of 1.49x.
- Bolmo's loop still needs a host round trip per byte (subword automaton); OLMo's feeds the token back on the GPU. That is part of the architecture's cost and is included.
- **Quantization rule for the matched ratio:** transformer blocks int4 in both models (Bolmo: local + global), embeddings and `lm_head` bf16 in both (Bolmo `lm_head` is 2 MB, OLMo's 0.21 G-parameter head is 421 MB, so "matched" means matched rule, not matched bytes).
- Stage C: `nsys` works here for kernel tracing inside graphs (`--cuda-graph-trace=node`), after two workarounds: `ptxas.exe --version` returns nothing under nsys on this machine, which made Triton raise "Cannot find ptxas.exe" (`tools/nsys_trace.py` returns the real output, taken outside nsys, for exactly that call; kernels come from the existing cache), and the target's stdout is swallowed. CPU sampling and context-switch tracing need administrator rights and were off. Tracing inflates host-side gaps, so gap figures come from untraced replay timings (below).
- Incident: after the speed phase my script was meant to stop, but `pkill` does not reach Windows processes, so a CUTE evaluation kept running during my first node-floor and sync-cost runs (their output was visibly 2-3x slow). I killed it, deleted its partial result, and rerun both measurements on an idle GPU (`utilization 0-2%`); only those reruns are reported. The speed runs had finished before any evaluation started.

### Verdicts

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Numbers |
|---|---|---|
| **H17** matched kernels/loop/rule (blocks int4, embeddings + head bf16): ratio 1.3-1.6x | **Refuted** | Bolmo all-int4 2.001 / OLMo int4 `blocks` 1.785 = **1.12x** (runs 1.12x and 1.12x) |
| **H18** OLMo head also int4: ratio >= 1.5x | **Refuted, narrowly** | 2.001 / 1.351 = **1.48x** (run-wise 1.47x-1.49x) |
| **H19** local int4 + global int8: <= 2.6 ms/byte, bpb < +2%, CUTE drop < 3 points (point) | **Supported** | 2.492 ms/byte (2.497, 2.486); bpb +1.20% (CI +1.09 to +1.32); CUTE-1500 -2.4 points (CI -3.7 to -1.0), old-500 subset -1.8 |
| **H20** >= 0.6 ms/byte per-kernel overhead; sync + its memcpy < 0.3 ms; state traffic < 0.1 ms | **Supported** (all three parts) | short-kernel GPU time 1.19 ms + in-graph gaps 0.12 ms = **1.31 ms/byte**; sync + D2H 0.03-0.05 ms/byte; analytic state traffic 21.1 MB/byte = 0.05-0.07 ms |

The prediction text in the pre-registered section ("OLMo near 1.35 ms/byte, ratio near 1.45x, with its head in int4 ~1.15 ms and 1.75x") was off in the other direction from what the 1.05x suggested: measured OLMo int4 `blocks` is 1.785 (prediction 1.35) and with the head in int4 1.351 (prediction ~1.15). OLMo's fixed part is lower than assumed (0.99 ms, below), and its 421 MB bf16 head costs 0.43 ms.

Weight MB/byte from `byteprof/quant/accounting.py` (bytes per patch 3.38, per token 3.39). "Previous task" cells were measured in the earlier session with the same weights, kernels and software; the speed numbers of all rows are from this session (bf16 3.985 here vs 3.978 there, all-int4 2.001 vs 1.992).

### Report points

**2. Table:** above.

**3. Ratios Bolmo / OLMo 2** (same session, same kernels, graph + minimal loop):

| precision | Bolmo | OLMo 2 | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 | 3.985 | 2.669 | **1.49x** |
| int8 (all int8 vs int8 blocks) | 2.666 | 1.903 | **1.40x** |
| int4, rule "blocks only" (head bf16 in OLMo, same rule in Bolmo) | 2.001 | 1.785 | **1.12x** |
| int4, OLMo head also int4 (OLMo best case) | 2.001 | 1.351 | **1.48x** |

The earlier **1.05x was too low by 0.07x under the matched rule** (1.12x) and by **0.43x against OLMo's best case** (1.48x). Its OLMo number (1.890) combined compiled torchao int4 with a bf16 head; the matched OLMo (1.785) is only 6% faster than that, so the compiled-kernel artifact was small. The main reason the 1.05x looked low was the comparison rule (OLMo's 421 MB head stays bf16 and costs 0.43 ms), not the kernel path. Under any int4 rule the ratio is much closer to 1 than at bf16 (1.49x), because Bolmo's extra time is largely weight-proportional.

**4. Fits** `ms/byte = fixed + k x weight MB/byte` (least squares, `tools/fair_report.py`):
- **Bolmo (8 variants):** fixed = **1.350 ms/byte**, k = 2.598 ms per GB/byte, R^2 = 0.990. Residuals: bf16 +0.05, local int8 -0.05, local int4 -0.11, global int4 0.00, all int4 -0.04, global int8 +0.06, all int8 +0.02, l4g8 +0.06 ms. The 5-variant fit quoted in the task (about 1.15) is the lower end; with the three int8 variants the fixed part is 1.35, and the residual pattern (int4 variants below the line, int8 and bf16 above) shows that one line does not describe both kernels equally: int4 gets more per byte than int8 (the fitted k mixes two kernels with different bandwidth efficiency).
- **OLMo 2 (4 variants):** fixed = **0.996 ms/byte**, k = 2.211 ms per GB/byte, R^2 = 0.963 (4 points, residuals up to 0.15 ms: the int4 `blocks` point is 0.15 above the line, `blocks+head` 0.09 below, so the fit is rough).
- **Fixed part:** Bolmo 1.35 vs OLMo 1.00: Bolmo has about 0.35 ms/byte more, which matches the local stack's non-matmul work (about 0.46 ms from the trace, below). k is similar (2.6 vs 2.2 ms per GB/byte, within the fit's uncertainty). So both models have a large weight-independent part, and Bolmo's is about 35% larger.

**5. Floor decomposition** (Bolmo all-int4, 2.001 ms/byte; `results/fair/floor_decomposition.md`):

| component | us per byte | share | how measured |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| matmul kernels (tinygemm; weight-proportional) | 800 | 40% | nsys trace, kernels per graph weighted by launches per byte at 3.38 bytes per patch |
| all other kernels (norms, elementwise, rotary, attention, mLSTM step, small copies) | 981 | 49% | nsys trace |
| gaps between kernels inside graph replays | 120 | 6% | untraced replay time (CUDA events, `tools/sync_cost_check.py`) minus kernel sum |
| outside replays (sync + D2H copy, host loop, launches, prefill share) | 100 | 5% | speed-run time minus replay time |

- **Non-matmul part = 1.20 ms/byte** (981 + 120 + 100), against the fitted fixed parts 1.35 (8 variants) and about 1.15 (task's 5-variant fit): **the fixed part is explained to within about 0.15 ms/byte (roughly 10%)**. Part of that residual is that the fitted fixed part also contains the bandwidth inefficiency of the matmuls at zero bytes and the prefill share, which a decomposition of a single variant cannot separate.
- **Kernel count: 574 kernels per generated byte** (encoder graph 64, decoder graph 217-225, global graph 985 per patch = 291 per byte; Nsight node-level trace, counted from `CUPTI_ACTIVITY_KIND_KERNEL`), of which **547 are shorter than 10 us** and take 1,187 us. Only 75 per byte are matmuls. The three small elementwise kernel families account for 35% of kernel time (`vectorized_elementwise` 237 per byte at 1.06 us, `unrolled_elementwise` 58 at 2.9 us, `elementwise` 62 at 1.8 us), `reduce` 38 per byte; the tinygemm matmuls are 45% of kernel time (67 per byte at 10.2 us), attention (`fmha`) 8.6%, the mLSTM step 1.2% (5 per byte at 3.7 us).
- **Node floor** (`tools/graph_node_floor.py`): a trivial kernel in a graph costs **1.0 us per node** (0.91-1.08, N = 100-1000, dependent or independent). 574 kernels x 1.0 us = **0.58 ms/byte** is the hardware floor of the current kernel count; the small elementwise kernels (1.06-2.9 us on average) are at that floor, i.e. they do almost no work and are pure per-node cost.
- **Sync:** pinned-memory read + event synchronize after a replay adds **25-50 us per replay** (piece C: 50 us, minimum 33; encoder 31, global 86 but noisy) = 0.03-0.05 ms/byte, with one sync per byte. The D2H copy itself is 2 us. In the trace, the NVTX `sync` range is 1.4 ms per byte, but that is mainly the wait for the GPU, not a cost of the sync.
- **Analytic mLSTM state traffic:** c is 1x16x128x128 fp32 = 1,048,576 B, n 8,192 B, m 64 B per layer, 5 layers = 5.28 MB; the step kernel reads and writes the state and the in-graph `copy_` reads and writes it again = **21.1 MB per byte**, i.e. 47 us at 448 GB/s, 62 us at 342 GB/s (best bf16 kernel of the Stage 1 table), 70 us at 300 GB/s. The trace sees 89 device-to-device copies per byte taking 88 us in total (copies of the state tensors, one per tensor and layer plus others), which is slightly more than the analytic time because every copy is a separate node at about 1 us, not because of bandwidth.
- **Not explained / uncertain:** the trace's own gap figure (485 us per byte between GPU operations) is higher than the 120 us from untraced replays; I attribute the difference to launch gaps between the three graphs (enc, dec, glob, about 30-50 us each plus CUPTI overhead) and tracing, but I did not separate them. The trace covers one prompt (`prose_en`, 4.4 bytes per patch) at 2 x 128 bytes; I rescaled its per-graph numbers to 3.38 bytes per patch, which assumes the per-graph kernel counts do not depend on position (the global attention kernel grows with the cache, a few us). The 100 us "outside replays" includes the prefill share of the wall-time metric, which I did not subtract separately.

**6. Is a fused per-byte kernel justified?** The measurement says: yes for the non-matmul work, and the ceiling is large, but it is not a local-stack-only job. In all-int4 the weight-independent part is about 1.2 ms of 2.0 ms/byte, made of 500 tiny kernels (547 of 574 under 10 us) executed at roughly the 1 us per-node floor. A kernel that fuses the elementwise/norm/rotary/state-copy chain of the layers could remove most of the 981 us of non-matmul kernel time and part of the 120 us of gaps, leaving the matmuls (800 us), attention (about 130 us per byte) and the recurrent step: an **upper bound of about 0.9-1.1 ms/byte saved, i.e. 45-55% of all-int4 time and 22-28% of bf16 time (3.985)**; realistic fusion probably reaches half of that, because norms, activations and the gating are not free when done in one kernel. The split matters: of the non-matmul kernel time, the **local stack** (encoder + decoder: 283 kernels per byte, about 370 us of non-matmul kernel time + about 85 us of gaps) is roughly **0.45 ms/byte**, the global model (873 non-matmul kernels per patch, 2.06 ms per patch = 0.61 ms/byte plus gaps) about 0.65 ms/byte. A per-byte fused kernel for the local stack alone therefore saves at most about 0.45 ms/byte (**22% of all-int4, 11% of bf16**), and a patch-level fusion of the global layers (or capturing them with fewer, fused kernels) is worth about as much; both are within reach of ordinary fusion (a Triton or CUDA kernel per layer), not of the weight-read floor. **For drafting:** a verified draft of k bytes executes one pass of the same kernels with k rows, so it amortizes both the weight reads and the per-kernel overhead; with 40% of all-int4 time in matmuls and 60% in launch-bound small work, drafting gets more benefit than the bandwidth model alone predicted, but only if the local mLSTM can process the k drafted bytes in one chunked call (it is sequential per byte today) and if the number of kernels does not grow with k. The expected value of drafting rises with the fixed part, but so does the competing value of plain fusion: a 2x gain from fusion would make the per-byte floor about 1.0 ms and shrink the amount drafting can still save. I did not measure drafting's acceptance rate, so no end-to-end estimate is made here.

### Problems and uncertainties

- Graph-based OLMo bf16 (2.669) is slower than compiled OLMo (2.544); the "fair" ratio is therefore a ratio between two eager-kernel graph loops, not between two best-effort implementations. A fused OLMo (compile + fused int4) would lower its numbers and raise the ratios (Bolmo bf16 / compiled OLMo = 1.57x).
- H17 and H18 are decided by one 5-prompt set at 256 bytes; run-to-run pair differences are 0.0-1.1%, so the 1.48x of H18 is clearly below 1.5 only in the point estimate (a 1.3% error moves it to 1.5).
- Quality: OLMo CUTE was not run. Bolmo's bpb contains the boundary flags. CUTE CIs are +-2.4 points at n = 1,500 (paired difference CIs are narrower, 0.6-1.4 points).
- The int8 results use my Triton kernel (per-row scales); local int4 + global int8 mixes two schemes, so its quality is dominated by the int4 local part (+1.20% bpb, identical to local int4 alone).
- The floor decomposition rests on one traced prompt and one variant (all-int4). The bf16 decomposition was not traced; I assume the non-matmul part is the same there (the fit supports that).
- Nsight limitations: no CPU sampling or context-switch data (administrator rights), so host-side causes of gaps (launch latency, Python) are inferred, not observed. The ptxas-version workaround touches only the one `ptxas --version` call.
- Colab notebook not updated or run; `tracegaps.py` was not used because `nsys` worked on Windows.


## Drafting

Task: `TASK_drafting.md`. Builds on `## Fair ratio, global int8, floor` (Bolmo bf16 3.985, all int4 2.001 ms/byte; OLMo 2 graph bf16 2.669).

### Pre-registered hypotheses and predictions (copied verbatim before any Stage 0 measurement)

Copy everything in this section **verbatim** into a new `## Drafting` section
of `FINDINGS.md` **before** any Stage 0 measurement. Never edit it afterwards.
Mark each hypothesis supported, refuted or not tested, with numbers.

My rough predictions, for the record: about 1.7 emitted bytes per local pass
with the combined drafter, a 1.2–1.5x verify cost at K = 4, about 10% end-to-end
gain in bf16 and about 6% in all-int4, about 20% with an oracle drafter, and a
byte tax that does not shrink once OLMo 2 drafts too.

- **H21 (acceptance):** with the combined drafter (context lookup, then corpus
  n-gram) at its best K ≤ 8, Bolmo emits at least 1.6 bytes per local pass on the
  extended prompt set at 256 new bytes. Code and JSON prompts score higher than
  prose; German scores lowest.
- **H22 (oracle bound):** an oracle drafter (the true next bytes) reaches at
  least 3.0 emitted bytes per local pass at K = 8.
- **H23 (verify cost):** one graphed round with K = 4 (A_4 + C_4) costs at most
  1.5x a K = 0 round in all-int4, and at most 1.3x in bf16.
- **H24 (end-to-end):** on the default prompts at 256 new bytes, the best real
  drafter is at least 8% faster than the minimal loop in bf16 and at least 5%
  faster in all-int4.
- **H25 (cost model):** measured end-to-end gains lie within ±30% (relative) of
  the Stage 1 predictions.
- **H26 (exactness):** in fp32, drafted output is byte-identical to the
  non-drafted minimal loop for all prompts and lengths, and the toy-model test
  (Stage 2) is exact. In bf16 and int4, first divergences are reported without a
  threshold.
- **H27 (byte tax):** with both models drafting (same drafter family, each on
  its own units), the Bolmo/OLMo 2 ratio at bf16 does not fall by more than
  0.05 below the same-session non-drafted ratio.
- **H28 (multi-patch, simulation):** multi-patch drafting with copied boundary
  flags raises emitted bytes per **global** pass by at least 1.3x over one patch
  per global pass.

### Stage 0: offline simulation (CPU only, on the recorded bf16 ground truth)

Code: `byteprof/drafting/` (`sim.py` round-rule simulator, `drafters.py` context/oracle/combined, `ngram.py`, `corpus.py`, `truthio.py`), `tools/draft_truth.py` (ground truth), `tools/build_drafting_corpus.py`, `tools/draft_sim_report.py`, `tools/make_drafting_prompts.py`. Tests: `tests/test_drafting_sim.py` (hand-made atom sequences: K = 0, full acceptance, first-byte mismatch, partial acceptance, boundary stop, flagged atom still emitted, the cap, multi-patch variant), `tests/test_drafters.py`, `tests/test_drafting_corpus.py`. Full tables: `results/drafting/stage0/report.md`, raw numbers `stage0.json`.

**Ground truth.** The existing bf16 minimal loop (`configs/fair/bolmo-bf16.toml`, graph-global + minimal loop) on the 5 default prompts and the 25 prompts of the new `prompts/drafting_ext.jsonl`, 256 and 1024 new bytes, atoms recorded as raw model ids (byte + 4, plus 260 for the boundary flag), `a_0` = the atom the prefill emits for the last prompt byte, and the prefill boundary mask of every prompt (`results/drafting/truth/bf16.json`). The 256-byte run is a prefix of the 1024-byte run for all 30 prompts. Two unicode prompts (`uni_1`, `uni_2`, about 2 bytes per patch) stop at 921 and 903 bytes: my recorder stops a run when the static global cache (512 patches) has 500 patches, so "1024" for the extended set pools those two at their available length. The fp32 references for H26 are recorded in Stage 2.

**Extended prompt set** (5 each of English prose, Python, German prose, JSON, mixed Unicode; the provenance is in the `source` field of every line). English, German and Unicode prompts were **written for this task** (they are original text and cannot be in a corpus); the 5 Python prompts are function openings from third-party packages in the venv (`packaging`, `filelock`, `networkx`, `einops`, `accelerate`; not the standard library), and the 5 JSON prompts are the beginnings of real config files of other models in the HF cache (Qwen3-TTS, Whisper). Prompt lengths 111-229 bytes. Pooled bytes per patch without drafting: extended set 3.79 (256 new bytes); the default prompts range from 2.4 (JSON) to 5.1 (prose) bytes per patch.

**N-gram corpus** (19,409,146 bytes, limit 20 MB): WikiText-2 raw **train** 10.91 MB (`Salesforce/wikitext` @ `b08601e0`), Python standard-library sources of the local 3.11 install 6.11 MB (349 of 516 candidate top-level files, a deterministic md5 subsample, without test suites, tkinter, idlelib, lib2to3, distutils), and the German side of `Helsinki-NLP/opus_books` de-en (every third row, 2.39 MB, @ `1f9f6191`). **No shared 32-byte window with any of the 30 prompts** (automated check `tests/test_drafting_corpus.py`; it also verifies that the checker finds a planted leak). The segmentation into flags uses the dolma2 tokenizer of OLMo 2 (a flag on the last byte of every token; 4,667,790 tokens, 4.16 bytes per token, 24.0% flagged bytes; 11.9 s); the same tokens are used for OLMo's token n-gram in Stage 3. N-gram tables: orders 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, direct-mapped hash tables with 2^24 slots per order, 8-bit check, count of the most frequent next atom; **build 22 s, peak RAM 1,035 MB** (fresh process, tables sharded by context key; my first unsharded build peaked at 2,284 MB inside the whole corpus pipeline, which broke the 2 GB rule, so I sharded it).

**Parameters** were chosen on the extended set only (256 new bytes, K = 4, pooled emitted bytes per local pass): context min match length 3; n-gram min count 2, max order 6; combined: min match 3, min count 1, max order 6. The default prompts were only reported. (The ranking among parameter values is flat: see `stage0.json`, `tune`.)
