**Reading the tables.** Acceptance is flattered by repetition: half of all 8-byte windows of the 256-byte outputs and three quarters of those of the 1024-byte outputs already occurred earlier in the prompt or the output, and the context drafter produces most of the combined drafter's gain (context alone 1.81, n-gram alone 1.55, combined 2.30 at K = 8). The same drafter accepts more at 1024 bytes (2.86) than at 256 (2.30) because the output has more to copy from, so the speed table at 256 bytes is the conservative end. The oracle is an upper bound only: with flagged atoms ending a round, even the true next bytes give about one patch per round (3.71 at K = 8 against 3.79 bytes per patch), so within-patch drafting cannot exceed the patch length; K beyond the patch length buys nothing.

### Checkpoint 0 verdicts

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Numbers (extended set, 256 new bytes, pooled) |
|---|---|---|
| **H21** combined drafter, best K <= 8: >= 1.6 emitted bytes per local pass; code and JSON score higher than prose, German lowest | **Threshold supported; the ordering claim refuted** | 2.30 at K = 8 (best K; 2.14 at K = 4, 1.87 at K = 2, 1.59 at K = 1). By type at K = 8: German 2.65, code 2.57, English 2.20, JSON 2.20, Unicode 2.00. Code is above English prose, JSON is not (2.196 vs 2.203), and **German is the highest, Unicode the lowest** (German outputs repeat a lot: 54% repeated windows; at K = 4: German 2.42, code 2.32, English 2.06, JSON 2.03, Unicode 1.95). Default prompts at 256 bytes (not used for tuning): 2.07 |
| **H22** oracle >= 3.0 emitted bytes per local pass at K = 8 | **Supported** | 3.71 (1024 bytes: 3.76; default prompts 3.28). The oracle is limited by patch ends, see above |
| **H28** multi-patch drafting with copied flags raises emitted bytes per global pass >= 1.3x over one patch per global pass | **Supported in the simulation, for K >= 4** | Combined drafter: 4.98 bytes per global pass at K = 4 (**1.31x** of 3.79 bytes per patch, 2.86 bytes per local pass), 5.52 at K = 6 (1.46x), **6.03 at K = 8 (1.59x**, 3.52 per local pass). Context alone 1.30x at K = 4. K = 2: 1.14x. Simulation only; Stage 4 is gated on Stage 1 |

Drafter host cost (propose + push per round, CPU Python): combined 9.5 us median (13.3 p90) at K = 4 and 13.2 us (18.2) at K = 8; context alone 2.4 us, n-gram alone 7.6 us at K = 4. That is below 0.5% of a ~3 ms round, so it matters only through the Stage 1 cost model (included there).

### Stage 1: verify-cost microbenchmark, cost model, gate

**What was built** (`byteprof/optim/draft_core.py`, `draft_kernels.py`, `bolmo_draft.py`; config `[draft]`, `configs/draft/*.toml`). One CUDA graph per K in {1, 2, 4, 6, 8} for the encoder (`A_K`) and two for the decoder (`C_K` without / with a new patch; `C_K` includes `model.norm`, `lm_head`, argmax, the acceptance and the state commit); K = 0 uses the unchanged minimal-loop graphs. The same kernels as the minimal loop: the projections, MLPs, norms, embeddings and the (int4 tinygemm) linears run batched over the K + 1 rows; the mLSTM recurrence runs as K + 1 sequential launches of the very Triton step kernel the minimal loop uses (`mlstm_recurrent_step__triton_fw`'s kernel, called with output buffers I supply, so the state after every position goes into a history slot; bit-identical to the library call in `tests/test_draft_kernels_gpu.py`); acceptance (`cumprod` of `d_j == p_j`, with a boundary-flagged `p_j` never equal to a plain byte id) and the commit of history slot L into the current state (one small Triton kernel per layer, the slot read from the device) happen inside the graph, so the host sync is still one per round and rollback is an index. The int4 linears (torchao tinygemm) and the bf16 matmuls were used as they are; I did not write kernels for M > 1 (the int8 Triton GEMV falls back to dequantize-then-matmul for M > 1, which is why the speed work is limited to bf16 and all-int4, as the task asks).

**Verify cost** (`tools/draft_verify_bench.py`, `results/drafting/stage1/{bf16,int4}/bench.md`; GPU ms per replay, median of 5 trials of 1,000 back-to-back replays, spread between trials 0.4-1.6%; Task Manager and an idle Chrome were open, Ollama not running). "Round" = `A_K` then `C_K` (no new patch) replayed back to back, the unit of H23.

| K | bf16 `A_K` | bf16 `C_K` | **bf16 round** | ratio to K = 0 | int4 `A_K` | int4 `C_K` | **int4 round** | ratio to K = 0 |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 0 | 0.3240 | 1.1488 | **1.4760** | 1.000 | 0.1823 | 0.6135 | **0.8015** | 1.000 |
| 1 | 0.3752 | 1.3936 | 1.7682 | 1.198 | 0.2046 | 0.7435 | 0.9492 | 1.184 |
| 2 | 0.3799 | 1.4120 | 1.7830 | 1.208 | 0.2102 | 0.7646 | 0.9742 | 1.215 |
| 4 | 0.3918 | 1.4492 | **1.8384** | **1.245** | 0.2296 | 0.8257 | **1.0578** | **1.320** |
| 6 | 0.4030 | 1.4765 | 1.8820 | 1.275 | 0.2554 | 0.9256 | 1.1833 | 1.476 |
| 8 | 0.4162 | 1.5204 | 1.9293 | 1.307 | 0.2744 | 0.9998 | 1.2741 | 1.590 |

`B` (the global graph for one patch): 7.856 ms in bf16, 3.894 ms in int4. `C_K` with a new patch is 13-21 us more than without. The jump from K = 0 to K = 1 (+0.29 ms bf16, +0.15 ms int4) is the fixed price of the drafting machinery (history writes, acceptance, the commit of five layers, an lm_head over two rows); each further position then costs about 23 us in bf16 (4.6 us per layer) and 36-45 us in int4 (K = 1 to 4: 36, K = 6 to 8: 45 us per position), so the int4 matmuls do get slower with M; I did not isolate where. The weights are read once per round, so the whole local stack at K = 8 costs 1.31x (bf16) and 1.59x (int4) of a K = 0 round while it can verify 9 bytes.

**Cost model** (`tools/draft_costmodel.py`, `results/drafting/stage1/costmodel.md`):
predicted ms/byte = prefill share + sum over K of (rounds with K / bytes) x (`A_K` + `C_K` round) + (global passes / bytes) x (`B` + the `C_p - C_n` difference) + (rounds / bytes) x (outside-replays time per round of the minimal loop + drafter host time), where the rates come from the Stage 0 simulator on the truth of the same precision, on the **default prompts at 256 bytes**, with the real loop's rounding of a draft down to an existing graph size. Outside-replays time and prefill come from an instrumented minimal-loop run of the same session (`--instrument timing`, 25 generations): 62 us per round and 0.168 ms/byte in bf16, 53 us and 0.199 ms/byte in int4; its replay times agree with the microbenchmark (global 7.83 vs 7.86 ms per call, encoder + decoder 1.51 vs 1.48 ms).

**Validation at K = 0** (required to be within 5%): bf16 predicted **4.067** vs measured **4.003** ms/byte (plain minimal-loop runs 4.035, 3.963, 4.011; a second run in the first pair, 4.269, was 5.8% above its partner and was discarded; Task Manager was open) = **+1.6%**; all-int4 predicted **2.051** vs measured **1.998** (2.029, 1.967) = **+2.7%**. Both are inside the 5% target; the model overpredicts slightly, so it is not tuned to hit the measurement.

**Predictions** (default prompts, 256 new bytes; gain = 1 - predicted / predicted K = 0; B/round = emitted bytes per round; `k_max` = largest verified draft, the graphs {0, 1, 2, 4, 6, 8} up to it exist):

| drafter | k_max | bf16 B/round | bf16 ms/byte | bf16 gain | int4 B/round | int4 ms/byte | int4 gain |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| none (K = 0) | 0 | 1.00 | 4.067 | 0 | 1.00 | 2.051 | 0 |
| context | 4 | 1.63 | 3.587 | +11.8% | 1.56 | 1.815 | +11.5% |
| n-gram | 4 | 1.46 | 3.831 | +5.8% | 1.65 | 1.873 | +8.7% |
| combined | 2 | 1.78 | 3.572 | +12.2% | 1.81 | 1.770 | +13.7% |
| **combined** | **4** | 1.98 | 3.494 | +14.1% | 2.04 | **1.746** | **+14.9%** |
| **combined** | **6** | 2.07 | **3.475** | **+14.6%** | 2.11 | 1.791 | +12.7% |
| combined | 8 | 2.07 | 3.495 | +14.1% | 2.12 | 1.831 | +10.8% |
| oracle (upper bound) | 4 | 2.88 | 3.190 | +21.6% | 3.19 | 1.545 | +24.7% |
| oracle (upper bound) | 6 | 3.19 | 3.138 | +22.8% | 3.58 | 1.543 | +24.8% |

The best K was chosen on the **extended** set (same model on the extended prompts' rates: combined best at k_max = 6 in bf16, 4 in int4); the default-prompt predictions above pick the same operating points.

**Gate decision: PASS, continue with the combined drafter, K = 6 (bf16) and K = 4 (all-int4).** The best real drafter is predicted at +14.6% in bf16 and +14.9% in all-int4 (against the 5% line in either), and the oracle at +22.8% / +24.8%. These predictions are above my own pre-registered ones (about 10% and 6%), because the measured acceptance (2.0 bytes per round on the default prompts) is above the 1.7 I guessed. They are also flattered by repetition (Stage 0), so Stage 3 has to measure them.

**H23 verdict (verify cost):** **Supported.** A K = 4 round costs **1.245x** a K = 0 round in bf16 (limit 1.3x) and **1.320x** in all-int4 (limit 1.5x); K = 8 would cost 1.307x and 1.590x.

### Stage 2: the drafting loop and its exactness (Checkpoint 2)

`[draft]` config (`kind = none|context|ngram|combined|oracle`, `k_max`, `ks`, `min_len`, `min_count`, `max_order`, `k_ngram` for a per-source K cap, `truth_file` for the oracle). `kind = "none"` (or no table) never imports the drafting code; `tests/test_eager_untouched.py` checks that and pins `bolmo_loop.py` by hash (unchanged), and `bolmo.py`/`olmo.py` reach the new code only behind the `kind != "none"` guard. The adaptive-K option is only the `k_ngram` cap; I did not measure it. Bolmo code: `byteprof/optim/{draft_core,draft_kernels,bolmo_draft}.py`; OLMo: `olmo_draft.py` (one graph per K over K + 1 tokens, acceptance and the next round's inputs/mask updated in the graph, cache rollback = the position).

**Toy-model test** (`tests/test_draft_toy.py`, CPU fp32, no download): a random-weight latent-tokenizer model (fused byte+flag atoms, recurrent encoder and decoder layers with matrix state, a stateful subword automaton, recurrent global layer) decoded one atom at a time and by the production `scan_with_history` / `commit_state` / `accepted_length_t` / `rounds_loop` with random, all-wrong and all-right drafts, boundaries inside the draft, caps at every length 1-29, EOS in a round, unavailable draft sizes. All identical. I checked that the test has teeth: after strengthening the toy's state dependence, four injected bugs (commit slot L-1, always the last slot, acceptance L+1, automaton state at K instead of L) are each caught. GPU kernel tests: `tests/test_draft_kernels_gpu.py` (step-into is bit-identical to the library step; commit inside a graph).

**H26 (exactness).** fp32, Bolmo: the combined, context, n-gram and oracle drafters (K up to 8) give atoms (bytes **and** boundary flags) identical to the fp32 minimal loop for all 30 prompts at 256 and 1024 bytes (240 of 240 runs; `results/drafting/exact/fp32-*.json`). The fp32 minimal loop equals fp32 eager for 27 of 30 prompts; the other three differ only in length (two runs stopped at 921/903 bytes by my recorder) or in the final incomplete UTF-8 byte (`uni_3`). OLMo 2, fp32, combined K = 8: 60/60 identical. **bf16 and all-int4 (reported, no threshold):** Bolmo bf16 K = 6 (combined): 44/60 prompt-length runs identical, 9 of 30 prompts diverge (first divergence at byte 24-336); Bolmo all-int4 K = 4: 42/60, 9 prompts (29-243). On the 5 default prompts bf16 diverges on 2 (`code_py` 24, `prose_de` 101) and int4 on none. OLMo bf16 K = 8: 36/60, 15 prompts (token 1-156), earlier and more often than Bolmo. These are not logic errors (fp32 is exact): at **every** divergence the top-2 logit gap of the non-drafted model is 0-0.125 (bf16/int4 resolution) while the median gap is 2-7 (`tools/draft_margin_check.py`, `margins-*.json`), i.e. a batched M > 1 matmul rounds differently from M = 1 at a near-tie. **H26 supported** (fp32 exact, toy test exact).

### Stage 3: speed (default prompts, 256 new bytes, 25 generations per run, two runs per cell, second run in reverse order)

Bolmo (`results/drafting/stage3/speed_bolmo.md`; bytes/s from the mean; the K the extended-set cost model picked is bold; Task Manager open, Ollama off). Speedup = ms/byte of `none` / ms/byte of the cell.

| precision | cell | runs | **ms/byte** | bytes/s | rounds/byte | bytes/round | **speedup** | predicted gain | measured gain | measured / predicted |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 | minimal loop (`none`) | 3.945, 3.965 | **3.955** | 253 | - | - | 1.000x | - | - | - |
| bf16 | context K=4 | 3.468, 3.479 | 3.473 | 288 | 0.605 | 1.65 | 1.139x | +11.8% | +12.2% | 1.03 |
| bf16 | combined K=4 | 3.384, 3.400 | 3.392 | 295 | 0.492 | 2.03 | 1.166x | +14.1% | +14.2% | 1.01 |
| bf16 | **combined K=6** | 3.297, 3.381 | **3.339** | 300 | 0.470 | 2.13 | **1.185x** | +14.6% | +15.6% | **1.07** |
| bf16 | combined K=8 | 2.843, 2.831 | 2.837 | 352 | 0.409 | 2.44 | 1.394x | +14.1% | +28.3% | 2.01 (see below) |
| bf16 | oracle K=6 (upper bound only) | 3.026, 2.974 | 3.000 | 333 | 0.311 | 3.22 | 1.318x | +22.8% | +24.2% | 1.06 |
| int4 | minimal loop (`none`) | 1.994, 1.994 | **1.994** | 502 | - | - | 1.000x | - | - | - |
| int4 | context K=4 | 1.762, 1.764 | 1.763 | 567 | 0.634 | 1.58 | 1.130x | +11.5% | +11.5% | 1.00 |
| int4 | combined K=2 | 1.744, 1.744 | 1.744 | 573 | 0.552 | 1.81 | 1.143x | +13.7% | +12.5% | 0.91 |
| int4 | **combined K=4** | 1.703, 1.704 | **1.703** | 587 | 0.490 | 2.04 | **1.170x** | +14.9% | +14.6% | **0.98** |
| int4 | combined K=6 | 1.773, 1.762 | 1.767 | 566 | 0.475 | 2.11 | 1.128x | +12.7% | +11.4% | 0.89 |
| int4 | oracle K=4 (upper bound only) | 1.503, 1.507 | 1.505 | 664 | 0.313 | 3.19 | 1.324x | +24.7% | +24.5% | 0.99 |

All pair differences are 0.0-2.6% (rule: rerun above 5%). The bf16 oracle was first run with the minimal loop's output as truth and was **invalid** (3.420 ms/byte, 0.52 rounds per byte): in bf16 the drafting loop's output diverges from the minimal loop after a near-tie and the stale truth then drafts garbage. I replaced it by a self-consistent truth, the fixed point of the loop's own output (`tools/draft_oracle_truth.py`: converged in 2 iterations in bf16 where two prompts changed, immediately in int4); the stale runs are kept in `results/drafting/speed/bf16/stale_oracle_before_fixedpoint/`. **Text effect:** in bf16 the output of the drafted loop is not always the minimal loop's, so a speed ratio mixes a speed and a text effect. K = 6 and K = 4 outputs equal the minimal loop's on 3 of 5 prompts (`code_py`, `prose_de` differ); on those 3 prompts the K = 6 speedup is 1.116x. The K = 8 cell is an outlier for exactly this reason: its `json_struct` output diverged at byte 88 into a much more repetitive text (2.94 bytes per round against 1.58), which produced 28% instead of the predicted 14%; I report it, I do not use it. In all-int4 all 25 outputs are identical to the minimal loop for every cell but one (`context K=4`, one prompt). Per-prompt tables: `speed_bolmo.md`.

**Acceptance on the extended set** (30-prompt exactness runs, real loop): bf16 K = 6 combined 2.24 emitted bytes per round at 256 and 2.77 at 1024; int4 K = 4: 2.14 and 2.68 (simulation: 2.25 / 2.86 and 2.12 / 2.54).

**OLMo 2 with the same drafter family on token ids** (`results/drafting/stage3/olmo_sim.md`, `speed_olmo.md`; parameters tuned on the extended set: context min match 1 token, n-gram min count 1, order <= 3; verify cost of one graph over K + 1 tokens: 9.27 ms for K = 0, 9.37 / 9.40 / 9.88 / 9.93 / 10.01 ms for K = 1 / 2 / 4 / 6 / 8, i.e. only 8% more for 9 tokens; best K = 8 by simulated tokens per round divided by that cost):

| cell | runs ms/byte | **ms/byte** | rounds/byte | tokens per round | speedup |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| minimal graph loop (`none`) | 2.671, 2.690 | **2.681** | - | 1 | 1.000x |
| context K=8 | 1.497, 1.492 | 1.495 | 0.142 | | 1.793x |
| combined K=2 | 1.662, 1.633 | 1.647 | 0.160 | | 1.627x |
| combined K=4 | 1.505, 1.504 | 1.505 | 0.140 | | 1.781x |
| **combined K=8** | 1.421, 1.417 | **1.419** | 0.130 | 2.88 (60-run exactness set) | **1.890x** |

**Same-session ratio block (H27)**, all four cells interleaved, run 2 in reverse order (`results/drafting/speed/h27`):

| cell | run 1 | run 2 | mean ms/byte |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Bolmo bf16, minimal loop | 4.006 | 3.984 | 3.995 |
| Bolmo bf16, combined K=6 | 3.364 | 3.358 | 3.361 |

| OLMo 2 bf16, combined K=8 | 1.420 | 1.419 | 1.420 |
| **ratio Bolmo / OLMo 2** | | | **1.486 not drafted, 2.368 both drafted** |

### Stage 4: multi-patch drafting, gate not met, not built

Timing of the 16 global layers over P patches in one pass (`tools/draft_global_multi_bench.py`): bf16 7.71 / 7.80 / 7.82 / 8.26 ms for P = 1 / 2 / 3 / 4 (single-patch graph 7.71); int4 3.81 / 3.87 / 3.91 / 4.01 ms (3.80). Extended model (`tools/draft_stage4_model.py`, `results/drafting/stage4/stage4_model.md`): a round costs `A_K + C_K` plus `B(P)` for the patches it runs speculatively (a0's plus every flagged drafted atom, at most 4), with the Stage 0 simulation of copied flags. Predicted ms/byte, combined drafter, default prompts: bf16 within-patch 3.429 (K = 6) vs multi-patch 3.344 (K = 6) and 3.279 (K = 8) against 3.449 within-patch at K = 8: at most **4.9% lower**; in all-int4 multi-patch is predicted **6-10% slower** (1.888 vs 1.724 at K = 4), because the global model then runs in almost every round instead of in a quarter of the bytes. On the extended prompts it is up to 9% lower (int4 K = 8) but 2% higher at K = 2. The condition "at least 10% more gain than within-patch drafting" is therefore **not met** under either reading (ms/byte: <= 4.9% on the speed workload; relative gain: 1.29x at best in bf16 and 0.5x in int4), so I wrote down the prediction and stopped. The model leaves out the multi-patch decoder's per-position patch select, so it is slightly optimistic for multi-patch. **H28 is supported in the simulation only** (1.31x at K = 4, 1.59x at K = 8 bytes per global pass), it is not tested on the model.

## Drafting: report

**1. Hypotheses.** Copied verbatim at the top of this section before any measurement; prefix of this file diffed against the backup `results/FINDINGS.before_drafting.md`: unchanged.

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Numbers |
|---|---|---|
| H21 acceptance | threshold **supported**, ordering claim **refuted** | combined K = 8: 2.30 bytes per local pass (>= 1.6); real loop on the extended set 2.24 (bf16 K=6) / 2.14 (int4 K=4); German is the highest, Unicode the lowest, JSON is not above English prose |
| H22 oracle bound | **supported** | 3.71 at K = 8 (>= 3.0); limited by patch ends |
| H23 verify cost | **supported** | K = 4 round 1.245x (bf16, <= 1.3) and 1.320x (int4, <= 1.5) |
| H24 end to end | **supported** | bf16 K=6 +15.6% (>= 8%; 1.116x = +10.4% on the 3 prompts with identical output); int4 K=4 +14.6% (>= 5%) |
| H25 cost model | **supported for the chosen cells** | measured / predicted gain 1.07 (bf16 K=6), 0.98 (int4 K=4); all other cells 0.89-1.06 except bf16 K=8 (2.01, text divergence) |
| H26 exactness | **supported** | fp32 exact (Bolmo 240/240 over four drafters, OLMo 60/60, toy test); bf16 44/60, int4 42/60 first-divergence reports, all at logit near-ties |
| H27 byte tax | **supported, trivially: it rose** | ratio 1.486 not drafted, 2.368 both drafted (+0.88; limit was a fall of more than 0.05) |
| H28 multi-patch (simulation) | **supported in simulation only** | 1.31x at K = 4, 1.59x at K = 8; Stage 4 not built (gate) |

My pre-registered predictions: acceptance 1.7 (got 2.0-2.3, flattered by repetition), verify cost 1.2-1.5x (got 1.25-1.32x), gains 10% bf16 / 6% int4 (got 15.6% / 14.6%), oracle 20% (got 24%), byte tax not shrinking (it grew a lot).

**2-3.** Stage 0 and Stage 1 tables are above. **4. Speed table:** above, with the ratio Bolmo / OLMo 2. **5. Exactness:** Stage 2 section.

**6. Did drafting reduce the byte tax?** No, it increased it. Bolmo gets 1.17-1.19x from within-patch drafting because only the local stack (about 37% of bf16 time, 40% of int4) is amortized, while the global model still runs once per patch (about 60% of the time in bf16): its cost per byte stays at 2.3 ms in bf16. OLMo 2's whole model is amortized per accepted token and verifying 9 tokens costs 8% more than one, so it speeds up 1.89x. The bf16 Bolmo/OLMo ratio went from 1.49x to 2.37x. Drafting is a real, cheap win for Bolmo in absolute terms (+15%, exact in fp32, no extra quality loss beyond near-tie flips) but it is a larger win for the token model. The next lever is therefore the global model, not more local drafting: multi-patch drafting would amortize it, but the extended cost model predicts at most 5% over within-patch drafting in bf16 and a loss in int4 (global runs almost every round), so it is not worth building as specified; the other options are int4 for the global model (already in the quantization tasks, it halves the global cost) combined with within-patch drafting (measured here: 1.994 to 1.703 ms/byte), and a fused local kernel for the remaining 0.2 ms/byte round overhead (the K = 0 to K = 1 jump of 0.29 ms in bf16 is mostly machinery cost that a fused commit/acceptance would shrink).

### Deviations, limits, problems

- Prompts: English, German and Unicode prompts were written for the task; the Python and JSON prompts are real files (site-packages, other models' configs in the HF cache). Ground truth is bf16 minimal loop; two unicode prompts are shorter at 1024 bytes (recorder stops at 500 patches of the 512-patch static cache).
- bf16/int4 text differs between K and from the minimal loop on some prompts (near-ties), so speed ratios across K mix a speed and a text effect; I give identical-output subsets and per-prompt tables. The oracle needed a fixed-point truth (above). OLMo has no oracle cell (not required). The K for each precision was chosen by the cost model on the extended set, the other K cells are sensitivity checks.
- Only bf16 and all-int4 were built and measured; the int8 Triton GEMV has no M > 1 kernel (it falls back to dequantize + matmul), so int8 was not tested. The K=0 to K=1 verify-cost jump (fixed machinery) and the growth of the int4 cost with K were not isolated further.
- Host cost of the drafter (9-13 us per round) is included in the model. Sync count stays one per round (`host and device agree on L` is asserted every round, never fired).
- Noise: Task Manager and an idle Chrome were open; one bf16 minimal-loop run (4.269) was 5.8% above its partner and was discarded; all other pairs are within 2.6%. The 10-minute limit set by the user in the middle of the work stopped one batch script; every later job was run in pieces below 10 minutes (no result was lost, nothing partial was kept).
- Not done: the Colab notebook was not updated; adaptive K was only implemented as the `k_ngram` cap, untested; nothing was committed to git.

## Parallel local verification

Task: `TASK_parallel_verify.md`. Builds on `## Drafting` (combined drafter bf16 3.339 ms/byte at K = 6, all-int4 1.703 at K = 4).

### Pre-registered hypotheses and predictions (copied verbatim before any measurement)

Copy this section **verbatim** into a new `## Parallel local verification`
section of `FINDINGS.md` **before** any measurement. Never edit it afterwards.
Mark each supported, refuted or not tested, with numbers.

Predictions for the record: about 70% of the bf16 growth per position is the
recurrence and its history writes; a fused kernel cuts the bf16 cost per extra
position to under a third; end-to-end, within-patch drafting gains another
5–8% in bf16 and less in int4.

- **H29 (attribution):** in bf16, at least 70% of the round-cost growth from
  K = 1 to K = 8 is spent in the mLSTM step kernels and history writes. In int4,
  the int4 linears account for at least 30% of the growth.
- **H30 (fused kernel):** with the fused multi-step kernel and lazy commit, the
  marginal cost per extra position (all five layers together) is at most 8 µs in
  bf16, and a K = 16 round costs at most 1.25x a K = 0 round in bf16.
- **H31 (fixed jump):** fusing acceptance and commit removes at least half of the
  K = 0 to K = 1 jump (0.29 ms bf16, 0.15 ms int4).
- **H32 (end-to-end):** with the fused verification, the combined drafter at its
  best K is at least 5% faster than today's drafting loop in bf16 (3.339 ms/byte
  in the drafting task) and at least 3% faster in int4 (1.703).
- **H33 (exactness):** in fp32, outputs stay identical to the minimal loop for
  all prompts; the fused kernel's per-position outputs and committed state match
  the step-kernel path to within fp32 rounding (max relative error reported).

### Verdicts at a glance

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Numbers |
|---|---|---|
| **H29** attribution | **bf16 part refuted (near miss), int4 part supported** | bf16, K = 1 to 8: the mLSTM step kernels (which contain the history writes) are **63%** of the kernel-time growth (+112.6 of +179.2 us; 57% of the growth of the first-to-last span, +198.3 us), limit 70%. int4: the int4 linears are **57%** (+172.9 of +302.7 us), limit 30%; the step kernels are 38% |
| **H30** fused kernel | **Refuted** | marginal cost per extra position **10.6 us** in bf16 (K = 1 to 32; 13.8 us for K = 1 to 8), limit 8 us. A K = 16 round costs **1.338x** a K = 0 round (1.326x with L = 0), limit 1.25x. The recurrence itself meets the goal: 16.1 us per position (steps) became **4.5 us** (all five layers); what is left is the batched linears and elementwise work |
| **H31** fixed jump | **Refuted** | K = 0 to 1 jump 0.289 -> 0.259 ms in bf16 (**-10%**), 0.143 -> 0.115 ms in int4 (**-20%**); the limit was -50%. The jump is mostly cuBLAS/tinygemm choosing different kernels for M = 2 rows than for M = 1, not the drafting machinery |
| **H32** end to end | **Refuted** | bf16 best K: fused K = 6 3.330 ms/byte against 3.352 for `steps` K = 6 in the same session (**+0.7%**; 3.339 in the drafting task: +0.3%), limit 5%. int4 K = 4: 1.691 against 1.715 (**+1.4%**; 1.703 in the drafting task: +0.7%), limit 3% |
| **H33** exactness | **Supported** | fp32 Bolmo, fused, K up to 32: output identical to the minimal loop on 30 of 30 prompts at 256 bytes and 30 of 30 at 1024 bytes. Kernel vs T sequential library step calls (T = 1..33, every L): max normwise relative error in fp32 **5.9e-6** (outputs h, head dim 128 test) / **6.1e-7** (Bolmo's head dim 64), state **1.9e-7**; bf16 inputs: h 2.2e-6, state 0 |

My pre-registered predictions: about 70% of the bf16 growth is the recurrence (got 63%); the recurrence cost per position is cut to under a third (**yes for the recurrence alone, 16.1 -> 4.5 us = 28%**, but the bf16 *total* per position fell only from 23.1 to 13.8 us, to 60%); end-to-end another 5-8% in bf16 and less in int4 (**got about 1%** in both; the cost model had predicted 1.2% and 1.5%, so the prediction was wrong because I assumed the recurrence was a larger part of a round than it is, not because the model failed).

**A premise of the task was wrong.** The task's "Why" section says the state of a layer is about 1 MiB (c: 16 x 128 x 128 fp32). Bolmo's local mLSTM has head_dim_qk = 64 (q and k are 2048 -> 1024, v is 2048 -> 2048), so c is 16 x 64 x 128 fp32 = **512 KiB** per layer and about 1.2 us per position and layer at 448 GB/s, not 2.3 us. My `notes/bolmo_decode_loop.md` from the first task had the same wrong shape (128 x 128); it now carries an erratum. The fused kernel handles both head dimensions (one or two blocks of 64) and the unit tests run both. The conclusion for the hypotheses is unchanged: the step kernel was 3.7 us per launch inside a graph, a launch-dominated number.

### Stage A0: attribution of the round cost (no new kernels)

Method. `tools/nsys_rounds.py` replays the graphed `A_K` + `C_K` (no new patch) 30 times back to back per K (300 untraced warm-up rounds first), inside one NVTX range per K, under Nsight Systems with `--cuda-graph-trace=node`; `tools/nsys_rounds_analyze.py` sums GPU kernel time by name class. The kernel classes: `mlstm_step` = `recurrent_step_fw_kernel` (it contains the history write), `commit` = the slot-copy kernel, `linear` = every gemm/gemv/tinygemm of the layers, `lm_head` = the last linear kernel of the round, `elementwise` = norms, gates, residuals, embeddings, soft caps, argmax, `scan` = acceptance. The traced span agrees with the microbenchmark of the drafting task (bf16: 1.461 ms against 1.476 for K = 0, 1.989 against 1.929 for K = 8). Raw files: `results/parallel/nsys/*-classes.json`, all tables `results/parallel/tables.md`.

My first attempt synchronised after every single round; the GPU clock then dropped between rounds and kernel times were inflated by up to 1.7x (K = 1 round: 4.76 ms of kernels), and it ordered K by clock state rather than by cost. I discarded it and traced blocks of 30 back-to-back rounds after a warm-up. The trace of all K values at once also made the Nsight importer fail (`AnalysisFailed`), so the fused path was traced in two files (K = 0, 1, 4, 8 and K = 16, 32).

**H29 reading.** bf16, K = 1 to 8, `steps`: kernel sum +179.2 us; step kernels +112.6 (63%), elementwise +20.5 (11%), linears +38.9 (22%), the rest 4%. So the recurrence is the largest single part, but not 70%; the batched bf16 matmuls (cuBLAS picks larger tiles as M grows) and the elementwise work take a third. int4: +302.7 us, int4 linears +172.9 (57%), step kernels +113.9 (38%). The step kernel costs 3.7 us at K = 0 and then about 3.2 us per extra launch (16.1 us per position for the five layers), which is launch-dominated, as the state traffic (1.2 us per layer and position at full bandwidth) is smaller than I assumed.

**Decomposition of the K = 0 -> 1 jump** (`steps`; bf16 +330 us in the trace, +289 us in the microbenchmark): the machinery the task blamed is small: commit kernels +15.8 us, acceptance +2.3, step kernels +14.6, elementwise +27.5 (the acceptance, history and output copies). The big terms are the **linears +490 us, offset by -165 us in "other" and -23 us in the lm_head**, a net +302 us: at K = 0 the minimal loop's graph runs cuBLAS **gemv/dot kernels** (M = 1), at K = 1 the same layers run **ampere 64x64 gemm** kernels with 32-44 thread blocks (M = 2) that are slower per layer projection (about 27 us against 22 us for one group of 20 and 17 projections, 44 against 33 us for another group of 10; the 2048 -> 16 gate projections go from 3.5 us to 10.8 us each, ten of them). In int4 the same effect shows in the isolated linears (table below): the bf16 gate projections +72 us for the ten of them, the tinygemm linears flat from M = 1 to 2. So the jump is not "fixed machinery cost" as the drafting task assumed; most of it is the kernel library's choice for two rows instead of one.

**int4 linears vs M** (`tools/linear_m_scaling.py`, one graph of 20 calls, median of 5 trials of 200 replays; us per call, `results/parallel/linear_m_int4.*`):

| linear (in x out, weight type) | count | M=1 | M=2 | M=5 | M=9 | M=17 | M=33 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2048x1024 AffineQuantizedTensor | 10 | 6.44 | 5.87 | 6.69 | 8.50 | 13.28 | 21.59 |
| 2048x2048 AffineQuantizedTensor | 15 | 7.57 | 7.59 | 9.53 | 12.30 | 22.82 | 38.00 |
| 2048x16 Parameter | 10 | 3.54 | 10.77 | 10.95 | 10.84 | 11.21 | 12.23 |
| 2048x2816 AffineQuantizedTensor | 10 | 9.98 | 10.12 | 12.52 | 16.64 | 30.88 | 52.45 |
| 2816x2048 AffineQuantizedTensor | 5 | 11.39 | 12.46 | 15.04 | 19.22 | 34.88 | 58.27 |
| **sum over the local stack (us)** | | 370.0 | 443.9 | 519.7 | 640.4 | 1070.3 | 1724.0 |

tinygemm is nearly flat from M = 1 to 2 (370 -> 444 us for the whole local stack, of which 72 us are the bf16 gate projections), then grows linearly: +28 us per extra row from M = 2 to 9 and +45 us per row from 9 to 33, i.e. 370 -> 1724 us for M = 1 -> 33 (4.7x time for 33x the rows with the same weight read). So the int4 linears stop being bandwidth-bound at about M = 5; the bf16 gate projections (2048 x 16, left in bf16 by the quantization task) add 72 us at M >= 2.

### Stage A1: fused multi-step kernel and lazy commit

Code: `byteprof/optim/mlstm_multistep.py` (Triton: `multistep_fw_kernel`, `lazy_commit_kernel`, `accept_kernel`), `byteprof/optim/draft_fused.py` (wrappers), `draft.verify = "steps" | "fused"` in `bolmo_draft.py` and `config.py`. Reference versions for the CPU toy test: `draft_core.scan_without_history` and `draft_core.lazy_commit_state`.

- **Kernel 1** (`multistep_fw_kernel`, one launch per layer): grid (value blocks, 16 heads), state c, n, m in registers, a loop over the T positions with the library's formulation (gate math in fp32 and cast to the input dtype, fp32 state update, the DHQK dimension in blocks of 64 whose partial sums are added in the same order, `qk_scale` as a compile-time constant), writes only `h_t`. It never writes the state; the first program of each head copies the start `n` and `m` to scratch.
- **Kernel 2** (`lazy_commit_kernel`): recomputes c, n, m from the start state over positions 0..L, where L is read from the device (a dynamic trip count, inside the CUDA graph), from the saved k, v and gates of kernel 1, and writes the live state. c is updated in place (each program reads its tile before writing it); `n` and `m` are read from the scratch copy because the live ones are overwritten by one program while the other programs of the head still need them. The k, v and gate tensors of the encoder layer come from the `A_K` graph and are read by the `C_K` graph (kept alive through references; the graph pools are not shared).
- **`accept_kernel`**: one launch computes L and copies the predicted ids to the output buffer; it replaces the cumprod/sum/copy kernels (`scan` class 2 kernels -> 1).
- **Tuning** (`tools/multistep_tune.py`, graph of 10 launches, T = 1..33): the library's value block 64 with 4 warps gave 37.6 us for T = 33 and one layer; a value block of 16 with one warp gives **22.7 us** (0.69 us per position and layer), and a block of 16 with two warps is best for the commit (13.5 us at L = 32). I used those (`draft_fused.BV`, `WARPS_*`). The first, untuned version (block 64) was bit-identical to the library in bf16; the tuned one reorders an fp32 sum and differs by 2.2e-6 (normwise) in `h` in bf16. Round costs of the untuned version are kept in `results/parallel/bench/{bf16,int4}-fused` (K = 32: 2.129 ms in bf16 against 2.026 tuned).
- **What I did not fuse.** The task also lists `last_h`, the cache lengths, `bytes_since_boundary` and the automaton state index. None of them lives on the device in this implementation: the automaton and the boundary counters are host Python (one host sync per round, unchanged), the global cache position is advanced inside the global graph, and `last_h` is not read in the drafting graphs. So "acceptance + commit" is all there was to fuse. The five lazy commits are five launches; a pointer-table kernel could make them one (about 10 us); I did not build it.

**Tests** (`tests/test_fused_kernels_gpu.py`, GPU, skipped without CUDA; 7 tests): kernel outputs and committed state against T sequential calls of the library step kernel for T = 1, 2, 5, 9, 17, 33 and **every L**, fp32 and bf16, head dim 64 (Bolmo) and 128 (two blocks); the pass leaves the live state untouched; fp32 agreement; the pass + commit inside a CUDA graph with L changed between replays and the state updated in place; the acceptance kernel against the reference. **Toy test** (`tests/test_draft_toy.py`): every existing test (random, all-wrong, all-right drafts, boundary inside the draft, caps at every length, EOS in a round, unavailable draft sizes) now runs on both paths through an autouse fixture (`steps` and `lazy`), plus `test_lazy_commit_state_equals_the_history_slots_and_has_teeth`. Mutation check of the lazy path (ad-hoc script, not kept in the repo): committing L instead of L + 1 positions makes 11 of 12 toy runs differ from plain decoding and always committing every position 12 of 12; "recompute from the live state instead of the saved start state" changes nothing in the toy, because the toy's first pass never modifies the live state (an equivalent mutant there; on the GPU this is exactly the hazard the n/m scratch avoids, covered by the in-place graph test). I also re-injected three of the drafting task's four bugs on both paths (ad-hoc script, not kept): commit slot L - 1 (11 of 12 toy runs differ), always one fixed slot (12 of 12), acceptance L + 1 (12 of 12, on `steps` and `lazy`; the host/device length check fires). The fourth (automaton state at K instead of L) lives in `rounds_loop`, which both paths share; I did not re-inject it. Default-path guards added: `draft.verify` is validated, `ks`/`k_max` accept up to 32 (it was 16), `bolmo_draft.py` imports the fused code only inside `verify == "fused"`, and a subprocess test shows the default path imports neither the fused module nor Triton. Full suite: **169 passed**.

**Checkpoint A1.** Kernel tests pass; fp32 output of the fused path on Bolmo is identical to the minimal loop (H33 below).

### Stage A2: measurements

**Round cost vs K** (figure F6 data; GPU ms per replay of `A_K` + `C_K`, no new patch; median of 5 trials of 500 back-to-back replays, trial spread up to 1.2% in bf16 and 1.9% in int4; `tools/draft_verify_bench.py`, `results/parallel/bench/`). "fused" = the drafts left in the buffer by the warm-up generation (L small, as in the real loop); "fused (L = 0)" forces every draft wrong. The worst case L = K was not benchmarked end to end: forcing it by iterating the drafts to a fixed point fails because a boundary-flagged prediction ends the acceptance; in the kernel microbenchmark the commit at L = 32 costs 13.5 us per layer, about 2 us per position for the five layers.

Reading. In bf16 the fused path removes about 40% of the per-position cost (23.1 -> 13.8 us for K = 1 to 8; 21.9 -> 10.6 us for K = 1 to 32) and 5% of the round at K = 8 and 16% at K = 32 (2.411 -> 2.026 ms); it saves 0.034 ms per round at K = 1, 0.052 ms at K = 4 and 0.099 ms at K = 8. The curve is still not flat: after the recurrence is fused, **the batched bf16 matmuls dominate the growth** (linear class +118 us from K = 1 to 32, with a step of about 95 us in the round between K = 12 and 16, when M passes 16 and cuBLAS picks other tiles; the `steps` path shows the same step, +143 us). In int4 the linears are the growth (+150 us of the +202 us from K = 1 to 8), the fused path saves 3% of the round at K = 1, 8% at K = 8 and 14% at K = 32, and K = 16 costs 2.09x a K = 0 round. The number of kernels per round no longer depends on K in the fused path (275 for every K >= 1; `steps`: 285 at K = 1, 320 at K = 8).

**Cost model** (`tools/parallel_costmodel.py`: the model of the drafting task with the round costs above, the same Stage 0 acceptance simulation, K up to 32; L = 0 round costs for `fused`; `results/parallel/costmodel.md`). Predicted ms/byte on the default prompts, combined drafter, by `k_max`:

| k_max | bf16 `steps` | bf16 `fused` | int4 `steps` | int4 `fused` |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 4 | 3.433 | 3.404 | 1.708 | 1.683 |
| 6 | 3.416 | **3.376** | 1.748 | 1.713 |
| 8 | 3.437 | 3.386 | 1.788 | 1.743 |
| 12 | 3.473 | 3.399 | 1.898 | 1.831 |
| 16 | 3.541 | 3.445 | 2.057 | 1.966 |
| 32 | 3.681 | 3.493 | 2.494 | 2.316 |

The best `k_max` by the extended prompt set (the rule of the drafting task) is 6 (`steps`) and **8 (`fused`)** in bf16, 4 for both in int4; on the default prompts it is 6 and 4 in all four cases. Predicted gain of `fused` over `steps` at the best K: **+1.2% (bf16)** and **+1.5% (int4)**. Larger K does not pay for within-patch drafting even with the cheaper verification: acceptance saturates at the patch length (Stage 0), so in bf16 the predicted gain falls from 15.5% at `k_max` 6 to 12.6% at 32.
