Of the boundary rounds 40% carried at least one flagged draft atom (multi-patch rounds, 63% of all bytes). Bytes per global pass match the simulation (7.15 vs 7.15) and are about double P0's (3.4-4.1 on the default prompts). On the default prompts the real loop emits more per round than the simulation (bf16 3.05 against 2.77, int4 2.38 against 2.34) because its text differs from the recorded text where it diverged at near-ties (the same happens to P0: 2.44 against 2.08).

### 5. Exactness

- **fp32** (H51): `tools/draft_exact.py` against `results/drafting/truth/fp32.json`, which compares raw ids, i.e. byte and flag: 30/30 at 256 and 30/30 at 1024 new bytes (`results/p4/exact/fp32-p4-*.json`). Task A's fused P0 was 60/60 as well.
- **bf16 and int4** differ from the minimal loop on some prompts because batched (M > 1) matmuls round differently (as in the drafting task). Identical outputs: bf16 20/30 at 256 and 18/30 at 1024 (the drafting task's P0 K = 6: 44/60 over both lengths, P4 38/60); int4 21/30 and 20/30 (the drafting task's P0 K = 4: 42/60, P4 41/60). `tools/draft_margin_check.py` on all first divergences (12 prompts in bf16, 10 in int4, `results/p4/exact/margins-*.json`): the top-2 logit gap of the non-drafted model at the divergence is **0.0 - 0.0625** in all 22 cases (a bf16 ulp at these logits), against a median gap of 2.1-8.0 before it, and 0-8% of earlier steps had a smaller gap. Every divergence is a near-tie.
- Host and device agree on the accepted length in every round (the loop raises otherwise); the fp32 run exercised it on 60 generations.

### 6. Where the byte tax stands

With P4 the drafted Bolmo is **1.36x** the drafted OLMo 2 in bf16 (1.93 vs 1.42 ms/byte; it was 1.97x with the best within-patch drafting in the same session and 2.37x in the drafting task), while the undrafted gap is 1.48x (within-patch drafting had widened it to 1.97-2.37x, P4 takes it below the undrafted figure). What is left of the tax is the local stack and the global passes per byte: per byte of P4 (bf16, default prompts) about 1.1 ms is global passes (0.135 passes per byte at 7.4 bytes per pass, ~8 ms each, flat in P), 0.6 ms the 0.33 local rounds per byte (1.8-2.0 ms each, of which 1.44 ms is the K = 0 round that no K amortizes), and the rest prefill and host (0.17 + ~0.05). The global model is therefore still 55-60% of the time, and 7.4 bytes per pass is set by the acceptance of the lookup draft (3.05 bytes per round). The 8-patch cap trims 44% of the crossing drafts in bf16; whether it limits the emitted bytes was not tested. The next lever is the global pass itself: weights are read once per pass whatever P is, so int8/int4 global weights (the all-int4 P4 cell already runs at 1.456 ms/byte, 1.37x the int4 minimal loop's speed) with an M > 1 kernel for P <= 8 rows (the int8 kernel exists only for M = 1), or more bytes per pass from a better crossing draft (the oracle crossing lookup reaches 15 bytes per round in the simulation, 5x P4's).

Artifacts: `byteprof/optim/draft_gated.py`, `byteprof/optim/bolmo_gated.py`, `tests/test_draft_gated_toy.py`, `tools/{make_p4_configs,p4_cross_bench,p4_sim,p4_costmodel,p4_stage0_report,p4_speed_report,p4_ext_report,p4_ratio_report}.py`, `configs/p4/`, `run_p4_{stage0,exact,exact_lp,margins,speed,ratio,ext}.sh`, `results/p4/` (`stage0/`, `exact/`, `speed/`, `costmodel.md`, `speed_report.md`, `ext_report.md`, `h53_report.md`). Backup of the file before this task: `results/FINDINGS.before_p4.md`.

## Skinny kernels

Skinny weight-only matmul kernels for M = 1..33 (TASK_skinny_kernels.md). Hypotheses were registered before any measurement; results and verdicts follow them.

### Pre-registered hypotheses and predictions (copied verbatim from TASK_skinny_kernels.md before any measurement)

Copy this section **verbatim** into a new `## Skinny kernels` section of
`FINDINGS.md` **before** any measurement. Never edit it afterwards. Mark each
supported, refuted or not tested, with numbers.

Predictions for the record: a global int8 pass drops from about 8 ms to about
4 ms; P4 with int8 global weights and bf16 local weights lands near 1.35–1.45
ms/byte (20–30% faster than bf16 P4) at no measurable quality cost; all-int4 P4
gains about 10%; with OLMo 2 on the same kernels the ratio stays near 1.3–1.4x.

- **H54 (int8 efficiency):** on the global layers' shapes, the skinny int8 kernel
  at M = 1 reaches at least 75% of the measured device bandwidth (reference: a
  large device-to-device copy), and its time at M = 8 is at most 1.25x its time
  at M = 1.
- **H55 (bf16 small M):** on the local layers' shapes, the skinny bf16 kernel at
  M = 2…9 costs at most 1.2x the cuBLAS M = 1 time.
- **H56 (near-lossless end-to-end):** P4 with global int8 weights, bf16 local
  weights and skinny kernels is at least 20% faster than bf16 P4 (same session),
  with a bpb change of at most +0.1% against bf16.
- **H57 (int4):** all-int4 P4 with the skinny int4 kernel is at least 8% faster
  than all-int4 P4 with tinygemm (1.456 ms/byte).
- **H58 (fair ratio):** with OLMo 2 on the same kernels and precision rule
  (transformer blocks int8, embeddings and heads bf16) and combined lookup
  drafting at K = 8, the drafted Bolmo/OLMo 2 ratio is between 1.2x and 1.5x.
- **H59 (correctness):** every kernel matches a dequantize-then-matmul reference
  in fp32 within a documented tolerance for all M = 1…33 and all real shapes,
  and every bf16/int8 output divergence between drafted and non-drafted runs of
  the same precision sits at a logit near-tie, as before.

### 1. Verdicts at a glance

| hypothesis | verdict | numbers |
|---|---|---|
| H54 int8 efficiency | **supported** | skinny int8 at M = 1 reaches 77% / 87% / 91% of the 411 GB/s copy bandwidth on the three global shapes (limit 75%); M = 8 / M = 1 = 1.05 / 0.97 / 1.00 (limit 1.25) |
| H55 bf16 small M | **supported** | skinny bf16 at M = 2..9 over cuBLAS at M = 1 on the local shapes: 0.99, 0.96, 0.92, 0.81 (limit 1.2) |
| H56 near-lossless end-to-end | **refuted** (near miss) | P4 with global int8 + bf16 local: 1.588 vs bf16 P4 1.936 ms/byte = **17.9% faster** (needs 20%); bpb +0.002% (limit +0.1%, met). On the 10 of 50 rows with identical text it is 25.0% faster (prediction 25.7%): the int8 flips change the text and lower the bytes per global pass from 7.4 to 6.6 |
| H57 int4 | **refuted** | all-int4 P4 with skinny int4 1.633 vs tinygemm 1.462 ms/byte = -11.7% (needs +8%); the minimal loop takes 38% more ms/byte |
| H58 fair ratio | **refuted** | drafted Bolmo (g8 P4) / OLMo 2 (int8 blocks, combined K = 8) = **1.60x** (band 1.2-1.5x); minimal loops 1.53x |
| H59 correctness | **supported** (OLMo 2 bf16 gaps not measured) | all kernels within 6.5e-4 componentwise of the fp32 reference for M = 1..33 on all real shapes (tolerance 4e-3); fp32 drafted = non-drafted: Bolmo 30/30 (g8) and 30/30 (all-int4) at 256 bytes, OLMo 2 60/60; bf16: Bolmo g8 41/60, all-int4 34/60 identical, every checked divergence at a top-2 gap <= 0.125 (g8) / 0.0625 (all-int4) |

**The stated predictions.** (1) "A global int8 pass drops from about 8 ms to about 4 ms": it drops to 4.96-5.22 ms (P = 1..8; bf16 was 7.69-8.33). The linears do halve (5.6 -> 3.0 ms) but the other ~2 ms of the pass (attention, norms, rotary, cache writes) is untouched: **missed**. (2) "P4 with int8 global weights and bf16 local weights near 1.35-1.45 ms/byte (20-30% faster)": 1.588 ms/byte, 17.9% faster: **missed on the high side**; the variant with int4 local layers (g8l4) measures 1.430 (26.1% faster) at +1.1% bpb. (3) "All-int4 P4 gains about 10%": it **loses** 11.7%. (4) "The ratio with OLMo 2 stays near 1.3-1.4x": 1.60x (the cost model predicted 1.61x).


### Method in one paragraph

`byteprof/optim/skinny.py` holds one Triton kernel family: `skinny_mm(x[M, K], W, scales)` for M = 1..64 rows (more rows are chunked), bf16 activations, fp32 accumulation, bf16 output, and three weight formats: bf16 `[N, K]`, int8 per output channel (the quantization of the existing `Int8Linear`) and int4 with groups of 128 along K. Each program owns 32 (int8: up to 64) output channels, streams their weights exactly once with a software-pipelined `tl.dot` in the swap-AB orientation (the weight tile is the MMA's M operand, the few activation rows are its N operand, so M = 1..16 costs one 16-wide MMA column block and 17..64 one 32/64-wide block), and optionally splits K over several programs with an fp32 workspace and a small reduce kernel (chosen by `tools/skinny_tune.py` per kind, shape and row bucket; `byteprof/optim/skinny_cfg.json`). The int4 format is my own (documented in the module): asymmetric group quantization with a float zero point (`scale * (q - zero)`, scale bf16, zero fp16, 4 bytes per group like tinygemm's `scales_and_zeros`); inside a group byte j holds codes j and j + 64, and each 32-row tile of a group is one contiguous 2 KiB block, because a plain `[N, K/2]` layout read 64-byte row segments and ran at half the bandwidth (24 vs 42 us for 8192 x 2048 in an isolated test). The dequantization is two integer ops, a bit-cast into the low mantissa of fp32 128.0 and one subtraction per weight; scale is applied per group on the fp32 partial sums. `SkinnyLinear` (`byteprof/optim/skinny_linear.py`) wraps a layer; `quantize.kernel = "skinny"` selects it (default `"default"`: today's kernels, unchanged). Inputs with more than 64 rows and non-bf16 activations use the same weights dequantized for that call.

Device copy bandwidth (256 MiB device-to-device copy, read + write counted) is **411 GB/s**; the floor of a call is its stored weight bytes (incl. scales) / that figure. us per call; GB/s (stored bytes / time) in brackets at M = 1.

What the tables say. (1) Today's int8 path is only usable at M = 1: at M >= 2 it dequantizes the whole weight and calls cuBLAS (e.g. 452 us for 8192 x 2048 against 43 us at M = 1), so a global pass with 2..8 rows costs 29.5 ms of linears instead of 2.9. (2) cuBLAS bf16 is at the bandwidth floor for the big shapes at M = 1..3 and loses 15-40% beyond (8192 x 2048: 83 us at M = 3, 96 at 4..16, 110 at 17; 2048 x 8192: 87 at 2..16, 140 from 17), and the small local shapes lose 25-40% already at M >= 2 (2816 x 2048: 33 us at M = 1, 45 us at 2..16). The bf16 skinny kernel is flat in M (+1-2% at 16 rows, +6-18% at 33) and equal or faster on all six shapes, including M = 1. (3) tinygemm int4 is excellent at M = 1..4 (8192 x 2048: 26 us, 341 GB/s) and degrades from M = 5 (40 us at 9, 66 at 16, 147 at 33); the skinny int4 kernel is flat (44 us up to M = 16) but 1.7x slower than tinygemm at M = 1: it is limited by the dequantization arithmetic (112-204 GB/s of packed weights), not by memory. It overtakes tinygemm between M = 9 and M = 16 depending on the shape (8192 x 2048 at M = 16: 46 vs 66 us) and is 1.2-1.8x faster than tinygemm at 33 rows. (4) The int8 skinny kernel reads the weights once for all rows: 77-91% of the copy bandwidth at M = 1 and the same time up to M = 16; at 17 rows (32-wide MMA block) it is up to 25% slower (2048 x 8192: 56 vs 45 us) and at 33 rows (64-wide block) 1.6-2.0x slower on the big shapes. At M = 1 it is 0-35% slower than the dedicated GEMV of the quantization task on five of six shapes (8192 x 2048: 46.7 vs 43.1 us; 1024 x 2048: 9.3 vs 6.9 us), the price of one kernel for all M.

Tuning: the configurations in `skinny_cfg.json` come from `tools/skinny_tune.py` (grid over tile rows, K block, warps, stages, then split-K on the best split-1 candidate, minimum of 7 CUDA-graph trials). Isolated bugs and dead ends found on the way are in section 6.

### 3. Cost-model predictions and the gate

`tools/skinny_costmodel.py` takes the measured pieces of the P4 cost model (`tools/p4_costmodel.py`: verify pair per K, global pass per P) and replaces the linear part of each piece: new = measured - sum(baseline kernel time of the piece's linears) + sum(skinny kernel time), at the piece's row count (K + 1 rows for the 5 local layers, P rows for the 16 global layers, 1 row for the two projections), interpolating the tables linearly in M. Acceptance tallies are the bf16 / int4 P4 tallies (a different weight precision changes the text a little, which is not modelled, see section 4). The model reproduces the old P4 prediction exactly (2.213 / 1.458 ms/byte on the default prompts; the measured P4 numbers were 1.934 / 1.456, so absolute predictions run ~14% high for bf16); the last column rescales to the measured reference.

| variant | k_cross / k_within | bytes per global pass (ext) | ms/byte ext | ms/byte default | change vs reference | scaled to the measured reference |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 P4 (baseline, cuBLAS) | 32 / 6 | 7.2 | 1.982 | 2.213 | +0.0% | 1.934 |
| bf16 P4, skinny bf16 local + global | 32 / 6 | 7.2 | 1.803 | 2.015 | +9.0% | 1.761 |
| global int8 (skinny) + local bf16 (skinny) | 32 / 6 | 7.2 | 1.481 | 1.644 | +25.7% | 1.437 |
| global int8 (skinny) + local int4 (skinny) | 32 / 6 | 7.2 | 1.422 | 1.578 | +28.7% | 1.379 |
| all-int4, tinygemm (today) | 16 / 4 | 6.9 | 1.206 | 1.458 | +0.0% | 1.456 |
| all-int4, skinny int4 | 32 / 6 | 7.5 | 1.349 | 1.656 | -13.6% | 1.654 |
| global int8 (skinny) + local int4 tinygemm | 32 / 4 | 7.5 | 1.331 | 1.625 | -11.5% | 1.623 |

OLMo 2 with int8 skinny blocks: the 16-block verify pass drops from 9.40 to 6.77 ms at 3 tokens (the drafted K = 8 verify width); scaling the measured drafted figure 1.420 ms/byte accordingly predicts **1.023 ms/byte**, and the Bolmo (global int8 + bf16 local) / OLMo 2 ratio **1.61x** (unscaled model numbers on both sides).

**Gate:** near-lossless variant (global int8, bf16 local with the skinny kernels) predicted +25.7% vs bf16 P4 (needs >= +15%) -> passes; all-int4 skinny predicted -13.6% vs today's all-int4 P4 (needs >= +8%) -> fails. The gate is an either/or, so **the gate passed and Stage 2 was built**, with all-int4 included only to test H57 (predicted to lose).

Piece check (before the speed cells): with the g8 variant the piece benchmarks give B(P = 1) = 4.96 ms (model 5.11), B(P = 8) = 5.22 (5.33) and the verify pair at K = 0 / 8 / 32 = 1.35 / 1.53 / 1.80 ms (model 1.35 / 1.55 / 1.83): the delta model is within 0.1 ms on every piece (`results/skinny/bench/g8`, `results/drafting/stage4/global_multi-g8.json`).

### 4. Quality, speed and ratio

**Quality** (same 200,000 bytes and chunking, `byteprof eval --task bpb`; Bolmo's bpb pays for the boundary flags, so it is comparable only within Bolmo). bpb with 95% CI; the large-M path used by the evaluation dequantizes the weights, so these numbers test the quantized weights, and the kernels are tested by the reference tests and the exactness runs.

| variant | bpb | change vs bf16 | earlier torchao figure (same precision) |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| bf16 (reference) | 0.99354 [0.9785, 1.0086] | +0.000% | - |
| bf16 weights through the skinny kernel path | 0.99355 [0.9785, 1.0086] | +0.000% | - |
| global int8, local bf16 (g8) | 0.99357 [0.9786, 1.0086] | +0.002% | global int8 (default kernel): 0.99357 (+0.002%) |
| global int8 + local int4, own format (g8l4) | 1.00479 [0.9895, 1.0200] | +1.132% | local int4 (torchao) + global int8: 1.00549 (+1.20%) |
| all-int4, own format (all4) | 1.03830 [1.0231, 1.0540] | +4.505% | all-int4 (torchao): 1.04229 (+4.9%) |
| OLMo 2 bf16 / int8 skinny blocks | 0.94730 / 0.94720 | -0.011% | earlier int8 blocks (default kernel): 0.94720 |

CUTE (1,500 items): bf16 56.07% [53.7, 58.5], global int8 + bf16 local 55.93% [53.5, 58.4] (-0.13 points; earlier torchao-era global int8: -0.2). The own int4 format is slightly better than torchao's in this test (all-int4 1.0383 vs 1.0423, local int4 + global int8 1.0048 vs 1.0055), (a possible reason is the float zero point, not tested), but it is a different quantizer, so it is a quality result of its own.

**Speed** (default prompts, 256 new bytes for Bolmo / 72 tokens for OLMo 2, 10 warmup generations, 5 measured passes over 5 prompts, greedy; `tools/draft_speed.py`; two runs per cell, the second in reverse order, batches B1-B4 each in one session; every pair is within 2.0%, the 5% rule never triggered; no rerun was needed). ms/byte includes prefill and host time. Full table: `results/skinny/speed_report.md`.

| cell | run 1 | run 2 | mean ms/byte | rounds/byte | bytes per global pass |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 minimal loop (B1) | 3.901 | 3.979 | 3.940 | - | - |

Changes of ms/byte (reduction, mean of the two runs); "identical rows" are the rows (prompt and run) whose generated text is identical in both cells, which removes the text effect (a different weight precision or rounding changes a few near-tie bytes, and the later text can be more or less draftable). Rows: 25 per run, 50 per cell.

| comparison | new | reference | change, all rows | identical rows | new / reference on them | change, identical rows |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| H56: g8 P4 vs bf16 P4 | 1.588 | 1.936 | +17.9% | 10/50 | 1.095 / 1.461 | +25.0% |
| g8 minimal vs bf16 minimal | 3.045 | 3.940 | +22.7% | 20/50 | 3.087 / 3.936 | +21.6% |
| bf16 skinny P4 vs bf16 P4 (kernel only) | 2.069 | 1.936 | -6.9% | 20/50 | 1.924 / 2.101 | +8.4% |
| bf16 skinny minimal vs bf16 minimal (kernel only) | 3.357 | 3.940 | +14.8% | 20/50 | 3.757 / 3.907 | +3.8% |
| g8l4 P4 vs bf16 P4 | 1.430 | 1.936 | +26.1% | 0/50 | - | - |
| g8l4 minimal vs bf16 minimal | 2.800 | 3.940 | +28.9% | 0/50 | - | - |

| all-int4 skinny minimal vs tinygemm minimal | 2.768 | 2.007 | -38.0% | 0/50 | - | - |
| OLMo 2 int8 skinny K=8 vs OLMo 2 bf16 K=8 | 0.994 | 1.421 | +30.0% | 10/50 | 0.893 / 1.161 | +23.1% |
| OLMo 2 int8 skinny minimal vs bf16 minimal | 1.973 | 2.670 | +26.1% | 10/50 | 3.272 / 4.511 | +27.5% |

**Ratio block (B4, one session, same kernels and precision rule: transformer blocks int8, embeddings and heads bf16):** minimal loops Bolmo 3.021 / OLMo 2 1.973 = **1.531x**; drafted (Bolmo P4 with global int8 + bf16 local, OLMo 2 combined lookup K = 8) 1.586 / 0.994 = **1.596x**. In bf16, same family and same batch: OLMo 2 minimal 2.670 and drafted 1.421 ms/byte (the drafted figure is the earlier 1.420), so int8 skinny blocks speed OLMo 2 up by 26.1% (minimal) and 30.0% (drafted), more than Bolmo's 22.7% / 17.9%.

**Predicted vs measured** (change of ms/byte against the same-session reference; the cost model assumes the reference's draft acceptance):

| variant | predicted | measured, all rows | measured, identical-text rows |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 P4, skinny bf16 kernel only | +9.0% | -6.9% | +8.4% (20 rows) |
| global int8 + bf16 local (g8), P4 | +25.7% | +17.9% | +25.0% (10 rows) |
| global int8 + local int4 (g8l4), P4 | +28.7% | +26.1% | - |
| all-int4 skinny P4 vs tinygemm P4 | -13.6% | -11.7% | - |

The text effect is visible in the bytes per global pass: bf16 P4 gets 7.4 bytes per pass, g8 6.6 (lower acceptance after the int8 flips), bf16-skinny 6.1 (the skinny bf16 rounding diverges from cuBLAS on more prompts than int8 does: 20 of 50 rows identical), all-int4 tinygemm 5.5 and skinny 6.2. This is why the all-rows gain of the near-lossless variant (17.9%) is 7 points below its identical-text gain (25.0%) and below the prediction (25.7%).

### 5. Exactness and numerical error (H59)

**Kernel error against the fp32 dequantize-then-matmul reference** (`tests/test_skinny.py`, `tools/skinny_errors.py`): componentwise error max_ij |y - ref|_ij / (|x| |W|^T)_ij over all M = 1..33 and the six real shapes (random weights, std 0.02): bf16 6.01e-04, int8 5.79e-04, int4 6.46e-04. The documented tolerance is TOL = 4e-3 (about 2^-8: bf16 output rounding 2^-9 plus the rounding of the dequantized int4 operand, each relative to the sum of the absolute terms); the worst case over 40 random draws of the smallest layer (N = 1, K = 128, int4) was 1.8e-3. The tests also cover odd sizes (N = 1, 37, 50, 129; K = 256, 384, 640), empty input, 150 rows (chunked), split-K configurations up to 100 splits, input validation, and that output row i does not depend on the other rows (bitwise) within a row bucket. The metric was changed once, after a failure: the first version normalised by max|ref| (normwise) and failed on a 1 x 128 int4 layer in 7 of 40 random draws (up to 6.4e-2) because the single output nearly cancelled; the absolute error was within the rounding bound, so this was the metric, not the kernel (the componentwise form is the standard one for rounding-error bounds). The normwise error over the six real shapes had been 3.8e-3 / 3.8e-3 / 4.1e-3 (bf16 / int8 / int4).

**Drafting exactness against the non-drafted minimal loop with the same weights** (atoms = byte and boundary flag for Bolmo, tokens for OLMo 2):

| model, precision, weights | length(s) | identical | diverging prompts | top-2 logit gap at the first divergence (prompts checked at 256 bytes) | median gap over earlier steps |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|---:|
| Bolmo fp32 activations, global int8 + original local (g8) | 256 | 30/30 | 0 | - | - |
| Bolmo fp32 activations, all-int4 own format | 256 | 30/30 | 0 | - | - |
| OLMo 2 fp32 activations, int8 skinny blocks, combined K = 8 | 72, 288 tokens | 60/60 | 0 | - | - |
| Bolmo bf16, g8, P4 | 256, 1024 | 41/60 | 12 | 12 prompts: max 0.125, median 0.0625 | 5.92 |
| Bolmo bf16, all-int4 own format, P4 | 256, 1024 | 34/60 | 15 | 15 prompts: max 0.0625, median 0.03125 | 3.84 |
| OLMo 2 bf16, int8 skinny blocks, combined K = 8 | 72, 288 tokens | 40/60 | 13 | not measured | - |

fp32 activations with quantized weights: the kernels take bf16 activations, so `SkinnyLinear` dequantizes the weights to fp32 for fp32 inputs (the kernel itself is not in the loop of these runs; its numerics are covered by the reference tests above and by the bf16 rows). The runs verify that the gated drafting logic is exact with the quantized weights and the new modules; 1,024-byte fp32 runs were too slow (the per-call dequantization costs ~35 s per prompt) and were stopped, so fp32 covers 256 bytes only. In bf16 the divergences are near-ties as before: every checked divergence has a gap of at most 0.125 (bf16 resolution at logits of 8-16) while the median gap is 2-8. The OLMo 2 bf16 gap check was not run (the existing margin tool is Bolmo-only); OLMo 2 bf16 diverges on 20 of 60 prompt-lengths (earlier bf16 figure: 24 of 60).

### 6. Where the byte tax stands, what did not work, deviations

**Where the byte tax stands.** With the same kernels, the same precision rule (blocks int8, embeddings and heads bf16) and the same lookup-drafting family, Bolmo costs **1.60x** OLMo 2 per byte (1.586 vs 0.994 ms/byte); the undrafted gap is 1.53x. The bf16 figure was 1.36x (P4 vs combined K = 8), so the skinny kernels **widened** the like-for-like gap: they remove 26-30% of OLMo 2's time (nearly all of it is weight streaming in 16 blocks) but only 18-23% of Bolmo's, because Bolmo's remaining time is not matrix streaming. Per byte of g8 P4 (1.588 ms, estimated from the piece benchmarks and the round tallies): about 0.15 global passes of ~5.1 ms = 0.78 ms (49%), of which ~3.0 ms per pass are int8 linears (at 80-90% of the copy bandwidth, i.e. near their floor) and ~2.0 ms are attention, norms, rotary and cache writes of the 16 layers; about 0.35 local rounds of 1.4-1.6 ms = 0.53 ms (33%), of which the K = 0 round (1.35 ms) is a floor that no draft length amortizes; prefill, host and outside-graph work ~0.28 ms (18%). What remains, in the order of size: (a) the non-matmul part of the global pass (~0.3 ms/byte; many small kernels, so fusing a global layer's norm / rotary / attention steps is the next candidate, not another matmul kernel); (b) bytes per global pass, which int8 lowered from 7.4 to 6.6 through text divergence and which a better crossing draft would raise (the oracle crossing lookup reaches 15); (c) the local rounds' 0.53 ms/byte; (d) for int4, the skinny kernel's small-M speed: dequantization arithmetic limits it to ~44 us on 8192 x 2048 against tinygemm's 26 us at M = 1..4, so a hybrid (tinygemm for M <= 4, skinny above) or a CUDA-C kernel with tinygemm-style fragment-ordered packing would be needed.

**What did not work or is uncertain (failures are results).**

- *int4 skinny kernel, three dead ends before the shipped version.* (1) The plain `[N, K/2]` packing with per-element dequantization ran at 86 us (8192 x 2048) although a bare cast-and-dot of the same bytes took 42 us: 64-byte row segments halve the effective bandwidth; the tile-major format brought the bare kernel to 24 us. (2) A zero point applied through a per-group sum of x (`tl.sum` over the activation tile, so scale and zero could stay in the epilogue) cost 35 us per call (cross-warp reductions every group); replaced by a float zero point folded into the dequantizing subtraction. (3) An int16 bit-cast dequantization crashes the Triton 3.2 (triton-windows) compiler with an LLVM `ArrayRef` assertion when the packed tile is loaded contiguously; the shipped form goes through int32 and fp32 bit-casts. The result is still 1.7x slower than tinygemm at M = 1 (section 2).
- *bf16 skinny kernel in P4 is slower end to end on the full prompts (2.069 vs 1.936) although its kernels are faster:* its rounding differs from cuBLAS, 30 of 50 rows change text and the changed text drafts worse (6.1 vs 7.4 bytes per global pass); on the 20 identical rows it is 8.4% faster, as predicted (9.0%). Any change of kernel rounding is also a change of text, and full-prompt speed ratios of drafted loops carry that noise (as in earlier tasks); the minimal loop is not affected (bf16 skinny 14.8% faster).
- *The kernel-error metric was changed once* (section 5): the first normwise definition failed on a nearly cancelling 1 x 128 output; the test now bounds the componentwise error with a tighter tolerance. I did not loosen a tolerance; I replaced a metric that was wrong for cancelling outputs, and report both numbers. A second test of mine (fp32 outputs of M = 1 and M = 3 bit-identical) asserted more than cuBLAS guarantees and was relaxed to 1e-5.
- *Exactness runs.* The first bf16 drafted runs at 1,024 bytes crashed with "the static global cache is full (512 patches)" on prompts whose diverged text has denser patches; the truth recorder now stops at 440 patches (`--patch-limit 440`, the previous default was 500) and `tools/draft_exact.py` records `cache_full` instead of aborting. These are tool changes, not measurement-logic changes; the failed attempt's logs were overwritten. fp32 runs at 1,024 bytes were aborted after 6 of 30 prompts (about 35 s per prompt with per-call dequantization), so the fp32 claim covers 256 bytes (60 prompt-runs over two variants); the kernel itself is not in the loop of the fp32 runs. OLMo 2 bf16 top-2 gaps were not measured (the margin tool is Bolmo-only).
- *Identical-text subsets are small* (10-20 of 50 rows per comparison, one or two prompts), so the identical-row percentages carry an uncertainty of a few points; they are the right check of the cost model, not a precise rate.
- *The cost model* predicted every variant within 2 points of the identical-text measurement (g8 25.7 vs 25.0, bf16-skinny 9.0 vs 8.4; all-int4 -13.6 vs -11.7 and g8l4 28.7 vs 26.1 on all rows; OLMo 1.023 vs 0.994 ms/byte), but it cannot predict the text effect that changes the full-prompt result.
- *bpb and CUTE:* bpb is teacher-forced over long chunks, so it runs the dequantized-weights path (more than 64 rows) and tests the quantized weights, not the kernels; CUTE generates byte by byte and does run the skinny kernels (g8). The CUTE difference (-0.13 points) is inside the CI (+-2.4).

**Deviations from the task wording.** (1) The int4 format is my own (tile-major packing, float zero point), not torchao's `int4pack`; it is a different quantizer, so its quality was measured separately (slightly better than torchao's). (2) "fp32 activations with the quantized weights" runs through `SkinnyLinear`'s dequantized fallback because the kernels take bf16 activations; 256 bytes only. (3) The near-lossless variant's local layers go through the skinny bf16 kernel via a new scheme `bf16_wo` (accepted only with `kernel = "skinny"`). (4) The reference cells (bf16 P4 / minimal) were run in batch B1 with the g8 cells; g8 was run again in B4 with OLMo 2 for the ratio block (g8 P4 1.588 and 1.586, minimal 3.045 and 3.021: reproducible). (5) All-int4 skinny was built and measured although its predicted gain failed the gate, to settle H57. (6) K for the cells: g8, bf16-skinny and g8l4 use the bf16 P4 choice (k_max 6, k_cross 32), all-int4 the int4 choice (4 / 16); no K sweep was run per variant.

Artifacts: `byteprof/optim/skinny.py`, `skinny_linear.py`, `skinny_cfg.json`; `quantize.kernel` in `byteprof/config.py`, `byteprof/runner.py`, `byteprof/quant/integrate.py`; `tests/test_skinny.py`, `tests/test_skinny_integration.py`; `tools/{skinny_bench,skinny_tune,skinny_costmodel,skinny_tables,skinny_errors,skinny_speed_report,skinny_findings,skinny_mutation,make_skinny_configs}.py`; `configs/skinny/`; `run_skinny_{tune,pieces,speed,batch,quality,quality_all,exact,olmo_exact,margins}.sh`; `results/skinny/` (`stage0.json`, `stage1.json`, `tables.md`, `costmodel.md`, `speed_report.md`, `errors.json`, `mutation.json`, `exact/`, `speed/`, `eval-*`). Backup of FINDINGS.md before this task: `results/FINDINGS.before_skinny.md`. `tools/draft_exact.py` was extended (cache-full handling).

## Megakernel theory

Task: `TASK_megakernel_theory.md`. Hypotheses and predictions copied verbatim before any measurement; backup `results/FINDINGS.before_megakernel.md`.

### Hypotheses and predictions (pre-registered)

Predictions for the record: most of today's overhead sits inside short kernels;
counter synchronization costs 1–2 µs per phase; a prefetching persistent kernel
reaches 75–85% of copy bandwidth; a real local megakernel would make P4 6–11%
faster with bf16 local weights and 14–16% with int4; the bandwidth-floor byte
tax is about 1.3x at equal precision and 1.05–1.15x with an int4 local stack.

- **H60 (census):** with today's best kernels (skinny kernels where they apply),
  a bf16 local K = 0 step still spends at least 35% of its time above the
  weight-read floor, and at least 70% of that excess is inside kernels rather
  than in gaps between them.
- **H61 (sync):** on this card, with one block per SM, a phase synchronized by
  global-memory counters costs at most 2 µs, and dataflow waits (each block
  waits for at most 4 producer blocks) are at least 30% cheaper per phase than
  an all-blocks barrier.
- **H62 (streaming):** a persistent kernel running the local stack's chain of
  matrix-vector phases in bf16, with weight prefetch across dependencies,
  reaches at least 75% of the measured copy bandwidth, and prefetch adds at
  least 10 percentage points over the same kernel without prefetch.
- **H63 (prediction):** the cost model built from Stages 0–2 predicts that a
  local megakernel makes P4 at least 6% faster with bf16 local weights
  (near-lossless variant) and at least 12% faster in all-int4.
- **H64 (floor ratio):** recomputed from the real parameter counts and measured
  passes and rounds per byte, the bandwidth-floor byte tax (both models at the
  floor, same precision) lies between 1.2x and 1.4x, and between 1.0x and 1.2x
  with the local stack in int4.

### Environment and method

- RTX 3060 Ti (38 SMs; torch reports a 3 MiB L2), driver 621.01, torch 2.6.0+cu124, CUDA toolkit 12.4 headers, one GPU visible. Ollama was not running; the GPU was idle (1-3% utilization, checked with nvidia-smi before the main runs). No hardware, driver or system setting was changed; the display watchdog (TDR) was left alone.
- Benchmark kernels are CUDA C++ compiled at run time with NVRTC (`nvrtc64_120_0.dll` of the installed 12.4 toolkit, straight to a cubin for sm_86) and launched through the driver API from ctypes (`byteprof/bench/cuda_rt.py`); no MSVC is needed (`cl` is not installed), and no new package was installed. The kernels use torch's context and stream, so they can be captured in torch CUDA graphs. Code: `byteprof/bench/{cuda_rt,sync_src,sync_bench,stream_src,stream_bench}.py`, drivers `tools/mk_*.py`, `run_megakernel_stage*.sh`, results `results/megakernel/`. No decode loop and no earlier section of this file was changed (`results/FINDINGS.before_megakernel.md`; the prefix was verified).
- Safety: persistent grids are checked against the occupancy API (`cuOccupancyMaxActiveBlocksPerMultiprocessor` x SM count) before each launch; every spin loop has a wall-clock cap (globaltimer; 50 ms in Stage 1, 20 ms in Stage 2) that sets an error flag, after which all blocks leave. The cap was exercised on purpose in tests and once by accident (a counter-base bug in my first version of the kernel timed out on the second launch and ended in the error flag, without a hang).
- The stack modelled in Stage 2 has the real shapes and parameter count (180.2 M parameters, 360.35 MB bf16): one encoder layer, `out_projection`, `in_projection`, four decoder layers, final norm and `lm_head`; five dependent phases per layer (norm + q/k/v/ogate/igate/fgate projections; mLSTM state update with the real 16 x 64 x 128 fp32 state; out projection + residual; norm + gate/up with silu; down + residual) = 28 phases and 27 barriers per step. Weights are random. The norm and mLSTM math is simplified (no per-head norm, no convolution; stabilised gates and the matrix-memory update are kept), the same device code runs in every variant, and a torch fp32 reference of the same math is the correctness check. Only M = 1 (K = 0) is measured.

### 1. Verdicts

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Numbers |
|---|---|---|
| **H60** census: bf16 K = 0 step >= 35% above the floor; >= 70% of the excess inside kernels | **Supported (narrowly on the first part)** | traced round 1385 us vs floor 877 us (360.35 MB at 411 GB/s): excess 509 us = **37%** of the round; inside kernels 435 us = **85%** of the excess, gaps 74 us. But the inside part is not ramp/tail of the matmuls (matmul kernels 914 us, only 37 us over the floor): it is 238 small non-matmul kernels (371 us). |
| **H61** sync: <= 2 us per phase; dataflow (<= 4 producers) >= 30% cheaper than an all-blocks barrier | **First part supported, second refuted** | barrier **0.98 us/phase** (sync only), 1.25 us with the publication read-back; dataflow 0.89 / 0.94 / 0.95 us for 1 / 2 / 4 producers = **9% / 4% / 3% cheaper** (with read-back: 1.16 / 1.20 / 1.22 vs 1.25). |
| **H62** streaming: persistent bf16 chain with prefetch >= 75% of copy bandwidth, and prefetch adds >= 10 points | **First part supported for bf16 only, second refuted** | best persistent bf16 1123 us = 321 GB/s = **78%** of 411 GB/s (82% of this session's copy measurement of 393 GB/s). Prefetch variants change it by -1.1 to +2.1 points relative to no prefetch (limit +10). int8 / int4 reach 48% / 21% (instruction-bound dequantization in my kernels). |
| **H63** prediction: local megakernel makes P4 >= 6% faster (bf16 local) and >= 12% (all-int4) | **Refuted** with the measured kernel and with the byte extrapolation; **only the ideal-overlap bound reaches the lines** | near-lossless P4: **+4.3%** from the measured step (1.521 vs 1.588 ms/byte), +7.8% at ideal overlap. All-int4 P4: **+7.5%** from the step extrapolated by bytes (1.352 vs 1.462), +12.1% at ideal overlap; my measured (instruction-bound) int4 kernel would be -8.7%. If the saving per round halves for K > 0 rounds (they are 99% of P4 rounds) the gains are +2.1% and +3.8%. |
| **H64** floor ratio 1.2-1.4x (equal precision), 1.0-1.2x (Bolmo local int4) | **Not supported as stated** (narrow misses; depends on loop and precision) | drafted loops, equal precision: bf16 **1.23x**, int8 1.42x; with Bolmo local int4: bf16 0.97x, int8 1.24x (1 of 4 cells in range). Minimal loops: 1.42x / 1.43x / 1.44x (bf16 / int8 / int4), local int4: 1.04x / 1.19x. |

The pre-registered **predictions for the record**: *most of the overhead sits inside short kernels* - right (85% inside), but for a different reason than the theory: the short kernels are the ~240 tiny non-matmul kernels, not the ramp of the matmuls. *Counter sync costs 1-2 us per phase* - right at the lower end (0.98 / 1.25 us). *A prefetching persistent kernel reaches 75-85% of copy bandwidth* - right for bf16 (75-78% of 411 GB/s, 78-82% of this session's copy measurement), but so does the same fused phases as 28 separate kernels in a CUDA graph (below), so persistence and prefetch add nothing in my implementation. *A real megakernel would make P4 6-11% faster in bf16 and 14-16% in int4* - **wrong**: 4.3% and 7.5% (7.8% and 12.1% as upper bounds). *Byte tax 1.3x at equal precision and 1.05-1.15x with int4 local* - partly: 1.23x (bf16 drafted) but 1.42x in int8, and 0.97-1.24x with int4 local.

### 2. Stage 0: census of today's local step

Traces: `tools/nsys_rounds.py` (the graphed local pair A_K + C_K, 30 back-to-back replays after 300 warm-up replays, Nsight Systems with `--cuda-graph-trace=node`), analysed by `tools/mk_census.py`. bf16 = skinny Triton kernels, bf16 local weights (config `configs/skinny/bolmo-bf16sk-p4.toml`, K = 0 and the P4 operating point K = 6); all-int4 = torchao tinygemm (`configs/p4/int4-p4.toml`, K = 0 and K = 4), the best int4 path today. Floor = local weight bytes / 411 GB/s: 360.35 MB (bf16, from the real parameter counts: 5 layers x 34.15 M + two 2048 x 2048 projections + `lm_head` 1.06 M = 180.2 M parameters) -> 877 us; 95.7 MB int4 (4-bit + scale and zero per group of 128) -> 233 us. mLSTM state traffic (10.5 MB per round, 25 us) is not in the floor.

| round | kernels | kernel sum us | traced period us | floor us | excess us (share of round) | inside kernels us (share of excess) | between kernels us | kernels < 10 us (time) | matmul kernels (time) | other kernels (time) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 skinny K = 0 | 280 | 1311 | 1385 | 877 | 509 (37%) | 435 (85%) | 74 | 238 (371 us) | 42 (914 us) | 238 (371 us) |
| bf16 skinny K = 6 | 277 | 1493 | 1531 | 877 | 654 (43%) | 616 (94%) | 38 | 224 (418 us) | 54 (1071 us) | 223 (417 us) |
| int4 tinygemm K = 0 | 285 | 697 | 830 | 233 | 597 (72%) | 464 (78%) | 133 | 274 (587 us) | 42 (328 us) | 243 (371 us) |
| int4 tinygemm K = 4 | 287 | 957 | 1051 | 233 | 818 (78%) | 724 (89%) | 94 | 261 (606 us) | 54 (521 us) | 233 (402 us) |

Reading. (1) The traced period is 3% above the untraced benchmark of the same pair (bf16 K = 0 1385 vs 1347 us; int4 830 vs 791 us). (2) In bf16 the 42 skinny matmul kernels together take 914 us, **4% above the weight-read floor**: the matmuls themselves are nearly at the floor. The excess of 509 us is 238 other kernels (371 us: elementwise, norms/reductions, the mLSTM step, copies; median duration 1.3 us, so mostly at the ~1 us graph-node floor measured before), plus 74 us of gaps. (3) In int4 the matmuls are 328 us against a 233 us floor (95 us over) and the other 243 kernels take 371 us, gaps 133 us; here the excess is 72% of the round. (4) At the P4 operating points the matmul kernels grow with K (bf16 K = 6: 1071 us for 54 matmul kernels; int4 K = 4: 521 us for 54) while the other kernels stay at about 417 / 402 us. Splitting the excess as the task asks: **inside kernels** = matmul above floor (37 us bf16, 95 us int4) + other kernels (371 / 371 us); **between kernels** = 74 / 133 us. So the overhead is a **kernel-count problem (fusion)**, not a barrier/ramp problem of long kernels.

### 3. Stage 1: synchronization microbenchmark

One persistent block per SM (38 blocks x 256 threads, residency checked), N dependent phases, each: every thread stores one float, `__syncthreads`, thread 0 does a release-add (`red.release.gpu`), the next phase waits (`ld.acquire.gpu` spin, capped). Total launch time was measured with CUDA events (median of 25 launches x 3 rounds), then a straight line was fitted through N = 10, 20, 40, 80: the slope is the cost per phase, the intercept the launch cost (~45 us for the persistent kernels, ~30 us for cooperative / graph launches). Work-only (no sync) is flat at ~58 us (slope 0.002).

| variant | N = 10 us | N = 20 | N = 40 | N = 80 | **slope us per phase** |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| (a) barrier, one counter, extra `__threadfence` | 59.4 | 73.7 | 101.4 | 156.7 | **1.39** |
| (a) barrier, one counter (with read-back of a producer value) | 58.4 | 71.4 | 96.3 | 146.3 | **1.25** |
| (a) barrier, relaxed polling + one fence | 59.4 | 74.8 | 103.1 | 159.5 | **1.42** |
| **(a) barrier, sync only** | 55.3 | 65.5 | 85.0 | 123.9 | **0.98** |
| (b) dataflow, 1 producer (with read-back) | 57.3 | 68.6 | 92.2 | 138.2 | **1.16** |
| (b) dataflow, 2 producers (with read-back) | 58.1 | 69.6 | 94.2 | 142.1 | **1.20** |
| (b) dataflow, 4 producers (with read-back) | 58.1 | 70.5 | 95.2 | 143.4 | **1.22** |
| (b) all 38 flags polled (with read-back) | 58.4 | 71.7 | 97.3 | 149.5 | **1.30** |
| **(b) dataflow, 1 producer, sync only** | 55.3 | 64.4 | 81.9 | 117.5 | **0.89** |
| **(b) dataflow, 2 producers, sync only** | 55.3 | 64.5 | 82.9 | 120.8 | **0.94** |
| **(b) dataflow, 4 producers, sync only** | 55.1 | 65.4 | 84.0 | 121.9 | **0.95** |
| (c) cooperative `grid.sync()` (cooperative launch supported on this card) | 38.4 | 47.3 | 65.1 | 101.7 | **0.91** |
| (d) graph of tiny kernels (one node per phase) | 41.8 | 44.0 | 57.3 | 91.1 | **0.73** |

Reading. The cheapest counter barrier costs 0.98 us per phase, close to cooperative groups (0.91) and to one node of a CUDA graph (0.73; the earlier fair-ratio task measured ~1.0 us for a trivial node). The release/acquire round trip through L2 sets the cost, not the number of arrivals: letting each block wait for only 1, 2 or 4 producers instead of all 38 saves 9%, 4% and 3%, far from 30%. A redundant `__threadfence` before the release costs 0.1-0.15 us per phase, relaxed polling + fence is slower than `ld.acquire`. Reading a value from a producer after the wait (what any consumer must do) adds another 0.2-0.3 us. **Synchronization is not the problem: 27 barriers cost about 27 us per step.** The unmeasured part is the arrival skew: in Stage 2 the slowest block of a matvec phase finishes 1.9 us after the mean (median over phases), which a barrier turns into idle time for everyone.

### 4. Stage 2: streaming microbenchmark

28 phases, the local stack's real shapes, one row of activations, random weights; step time = median over 7 rounds x 5 trials x 20 CUDA-graph replays (variants interleaved). Weight GB/s = weight bytes / step time. Reference bandwidths in this session (256 MiB buffers): copy (read + write counted) 393 / 400 / 401 GB/s and read-only 425 GB/s; the percentage columns use the task's 411 GB/s. The asymptotic bandwidth of the matvec loop itself (one phase, 100-200 MB) is 428 GB/s (`results/megakernel/asym.json`); a single phase kernel has a fixed cost of 7.7 us (launch + ramp + tail).

**bf16 (360.35 MB)**

| variant | step us | weight GB/s | % of 411 GB/s |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| a: per-phase graph, 38 blocks | 1132 | 318 | 77% |
| a: per-phase graph, 76 blocks | 1126 | 320 | 78% |
| a: per-phase graph, 114 blocks | 1198 | 301 | 73% |
| a: per-phase graph, 152 blocks | 1144 | 315 | 77% |
| a: per-phase graph, 228 blocks | 1192 | 302 | 74% |
| b: persistent, no prefetch | 1153 | 313 | 76% |
| c: persistent, prefetch 80 KiB (end of phase) | 1165 | 309 | 75% |
| c: persistent, prefetch 32 KiB (end of phase) | 1123 | 321 | 78% |
| c2: persistent, double-buffered prefetch 80 KiB | 1169 | 308 | 75% |
| c0: persistent, state preload only | 1139 | 316 | 77% |

**int8, per-row scale (180.51 MB)**

| variant | step us | weight GB/s | % of 411 GB/s |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| a: per-phase graph, 38 blocks | 875 | 206 | 50% |
| a: per-phase graph, 76 blocks | 915 | 197 | 48% |
| a: per-phase graph, 114 blocks | 982 | 184 | 45% |
| a: per-phase graph, 152 blocks | 1133 | 159 | 39% |
| a: per-phase graph, 228 blocks | 1172 | 154 | 37% |
| b: persistent, no prefetch | 918 | 197 | 48% |
| c: persistent, prefetch 80 KiB (end of phase) | 931 | 194 | 47% |
| c: persistent, prefetch 32 KiB (end of phase) | 927 | 195 | 47% |
| c2: persistent, double-buffered prefetch 80 KiB | 944 | 191 | 47% |
| c0: persistent, state preload only | 936 | 193 | 47% |

**int4, group 128, fp16 scale (92.94 MB)**

| variant | step us | weight GB/s | % of 411 GB/s |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| a: per-phase graph, 38 blocks | 1015 | 92 | 22% |
| a: per-phase graph, 76 blocks | 1084 | 86 | 21% |
| a: per-phase graph, 114 blocks | 1150 | 81 | 20% |
| a: per-phase graph, 152 blocks | 1291 | 72 | 18% |
| a: per-phase graph, 228 blocks | 1370 | 68 | 17% |
| b: persistent, no prefetch | 1064 | 87 | 21% |
| c: persistent, prefetch 80 KiB (end of phase) | 1115 | 83 | 20% |
| c: persistent, prefetch 32 KiB (end of phase) | 1071 | 87 | 21% |
| c2: persistent, double-buffered prefetch 80 KiB | 1089 | 85 | 21% |
| c0: persistent, state preload only | 1068 | 87 | 21% |

Variants: (a) one fused phase kernel per phase in a CUDA graph (28 launches; this is already 'perfectly fused' - today's real step has ~280 kernels); (b) persistent, one block per SM, waits for the dependency before loading; (c) persistent, at the end of a phase each block stages the first rows of its next-phase slice (up to 80 KiB) in shared memory with `cp.async` and preloads the next mLSTM state slice, then waits at the barrier; (c2) the same, issued at the start of the previous phase into a double buffer; (c0) state preload only. All variants issue the first batch of weight loads before the input vector is read. For context, the existing Triton skinny linears alone take 0.93 ms (bf16) / 0.59 (int8) / 0.40 (int4 tinygemm) per K = 0 round, plus ~280 small kernels.

Correctness: the persistent kernel (without prefetch, both prefetch modes) and the per-phase kernels agree with the fp32 torch reference of the same math to a maximum relative error of about 1e-6 (bf16 1.3e-6, int8 1.4e-6, int4 3.0e-6 on logits; limit in the tests 1e-4), for one step and for two steps (state carried, n / m double buffer swapped, counter base advanced) and after capture in a CUDA graph. Reading: **the persistent kernel is not faster than the 28-kernel graph** (1123 vs 1126 us in bf16; 918 vs 875 in int8, 1064 vs 1015 in int4), and **prefetching the next phase's weights does not help** (the staged bytes just move into the barrier wait; per phase the DRAM time is the same). More blocks per kernel do not help the per-phase variant either. In int8 and int4 my kernels are instruction-bound (several shift / mask / convert instructions per weight, one block per SM): they reach only 48% and 21% of copy bandwidth, and more blocks per SM make them slower, so the int8/int4 step times below are properties of my dequantization code, not of the card (the Triton int8 skinny kernel reached 77-91% in the previous task).

Where the bf16 step goes (timestamps per block and phase, `results/megakernel/tsprof-bf16.json`, variant b): the ~22 matvec phases need 842 us at the asymptotic 428 GB/s; the measured step is 1123 us. The difference is made of 27 barriers x 0.98 us = 26 us, five mLSTM phases of 19 us each (95 us: a dependent chain of state load, update, reduction and store), and a per-phase loss of ~5-8 us (arrival skew 1.9 us + input-vector load and norm + first-load latency) x 22 matvec phases. The single-kernel fixed cost fitted in `asym.json` (7.7 us) is the same loss. A persistent kernel pays it as well; I did not find a way to hide it.

### 5. Stage 3: cost model, predicted ms/byte, floor ratio, gate

**Model.** `pred ms/byte = measured ms/byte - rounds per byte x saving per round`, with the measured cells of `## Skinny kernels` (default prompts) and `saving = today's K = 0 pair (A + C, untraced benchmark) - (megakernel step + extras)`. Extras = 3 small phases the benchmark does not contain (embedding gather and add, argmax / token write, boundary bookkeeping), 31 us. The saving is applied unchanged to every round, including K > 0 rounds (99% of P4 rounds): this assumes that a tensor-core M > 1 megakernel streams the weights once and adds the same marginal per-row cost as today's fused verify path. **That is not measured here** (Stage 2 is M = 1 only); the 'halved for K > 0' column shows the sensitivity. The host loop (`outside replays`, drafter host time, prefill) stays as measured.

| local step (bf16 / int8 / int4) | weight MB | 28-kernel graph us | persistent best us | stream floor at asymptote us | ideal overlap us | extrapolated by bytes us |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 | 360.3 | 1126 | 1123 | 842 | 963 | (measured) |
| int8 | 180.5 | 875 | 918 | 422 | 543 | 703 |
| int4 | 92.9 | 1015 | 1064 | 217 | 338 | 498 |

*Measured persistent*: the best persistent variant of Stage 2. *Ideal overlap*: stream floor at 428 GB/s + 27 x 0.98 us sync + 5 mLSTM phases x 19 us, i.e. the theory's promise that all ramp/tail is hidden; no variant reached it. *Extrapolated by bytes* (int8, int4 only): stream floor + the bf16 step's loss over its own floor (281 us), i.e. an int8/int4 kernel that is bandwidth-bound like bf16 is.

**Near-lossless: global int8, local bf16 (skinny).** Today's K = 0 local round 1.347 ms.

| megakernel step | step + extras ms | saving per round ms | minimal loop ms/byte (today -> pred, gain) | P4 ms/byte (today -> pred, gain) | P4 gain if the saving halves for K > 0 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| measured kernel | 1.154 | +0.193 | 3.045 -> 2.852 (+6.3%) | 1.588 -> 1.521 (+4.3%) | +2.1% |
| ideal overlap | 0.994 | +0.353 | 3.045 -> 2.692 (+11.6%) | 1.588 -> 1.464 (+7.8%) | +3.9% |

**All-int4 (tinygemm, today's best int4).** Today's K = 0 local round 0.791 ms.

| megakernel step | step + extras ms | saving per round ms | minimal loop ms/byte (today -> pred, gain) | P4 ms/byte (today -> pred, gain) | P4 gain if the saving halves for K > 0 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| measured kernel | 1.095 | -0.304 | 2.007 -> 2.311 (-15.1%) | 1.462 -> 1.589 (-8.7%) | -4.4% |
| extrapolated by bytes | 0.529 | +0.262 | 2.007 -> 1.745 (+13.1%) | 1.462 -> 1.352 (+7.5%) | +3.8% |
| ideal overlap | 0.370 | +0.422 | 2.007 -> 1.585 (+21.0%) | 1.462 -> 1.285 (+12.1%) | +6.1% |

**Fair floor ratio (H64).** Both models at the weight-read floor, bytes per generated byte / 411 GB/s, from the real parameter counts (Bolmo local 180.17 M, Bolmo global 1.0737 G, OLMo 2 blocks 1.0737 G, OLMo 2 head 0.2097 G; int4 = 0.53125 byte per parameter) and the measured loop statistics: minimal loop = 1 / 3.38 global passes per byte and 1 local round per byte for Bolmo, 0.275 passes per byte for OLMo; drafted = P4 (bytes per global pass 7.4 bf16 / 6.6 int8, rounds per byte 0.328 / 0.352) against OLMo combined K = 8 (0.130 / 0.125 passes per byte). Bolmo's floor includes 10.5 MB of mLSTM state traffic per round; OLMo's KV cache is not counted. Because both floors scale with the same achieved-bandwidth fraction, the ratio of 'equal megakernel efficiency' is the ratio of floors.

| loop | precision | Bolmo GB/byte | OLMo 2 GB/byte | **ratio, equal precision** | **ratio, Bolmo local int4** | (OLMo head bf16: equal / local int4) | Bolmo floor ms/byte | OLMo floor ms/byte |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| minimal | bf16 | 1.001 | 0.706 | **1.42x** | **1.04x** | 1.42x / 1.04x | 2.435 | 1.717 |
| minimal | int8 | 0.503 | 0.353 | **1.43x** | **1.19x** | 1.23x / 1.02x | 1.224 | 0.859 |
| minimal | int4 | 0.270 | 0.187 | **1.44x** | **1.44x** | 0.99x / 0.99x | 0.656 | 0.456 |
| drafted bf16 | bf16 | 0.410 | 0.334 | **1.23x** | **0.97x** | 1.23x / 0.97x | 0.998 | 0.813 |
| drafted int8 | int8 | 0.228 | 0.160 | **1.42x** | **1.24x** | 1.22x / 1.06x | 0.554 | 0.390 |

Measured today: Bolmo g8 P4 1.588 vs OLMo 2 int8 skinny K = 8 0.994 ms/byte = **1.60x** (`## Skinny kernels`), against a floor ratio of 1.42x in int8; in bf16 1.36x against 1.23x. Both models are far from their floors (Bolmo 35% and OLMo 39% of floor speed in int8); the weight-independent costs (norms, attention, cache, host loop) dominate both. The 'with local int4' columns are not a deployable setting by themselves (a global int8 + local int4 model has a different quality; see `## Local-stack quantization`).

**Gate (nothing is built here).** Recommend building a real local megakernel only if (i) the predicted P4 gain is >= 10% in either precision, (ii) sync <= 2 us per phase, (iii) streaming >= 75% of copy bandwidth.

| condition | value | result |
|---|---|---|
| (i) predicted P4 gain >= 10% | near-lossless +4.3% (measured step), +7.8% (ideal overlap); all-int4 +7.5% (bytes extrapolation), +12.1% (ideal overlap) | **fails** (best realistic 7.5%; even the ideal-overlap bound reaches 10% only in int4) |
| (ii) sync <= 2 us per phase | 0.98 us (sync only), 1.25 us with read-back | passes |
| (iii) streaming >= 75% of copy bandwidth | bf16 78%, int8 48%, int4 21% | bf16 passes, int8 / int4 fail (my dequantization is instruction-bound) |

**Recommendation: do not build the persistent local megakernel now.** Condition (i) fails: the near-lossless variant needs a saving of about 452 us per round to reach 10% of 1.588 ms/byte at 0.352 rounds/byte, the model predicts 193 us (measured step) to 353 us (ideal). What would change it: (1) a persistent kernel that actually hides the per-phase loss (~5-8 us x 22 phases), which would need either register-level cross-barrier prefetch with finer dynamic work distribution against the arrival skew, or a hardware feature I did not use (e.g. thread-block clusters are not available on sm_86); (2) an int4 dequantization that reaches the bandwidth (4-bit weights pay off only then); (3) an M > 1 (K > 0) megakernel, since 99% of P4 rounds verify 7+ rows. What the data does support is the cheaper cousin: **fuse the ~240 small non-matmul kernels** of the local round (their 371-397 us and the gaps are the whole excess), e.g. as the 28 fused phase kernels modelled here in a CUDA graph (1.13 ms against 1.35-1.39 ms today in bf16: a 0.2 ms saving per round without any persistence or spin-waiting, if the real round fuses as well as the model).

### 6. Engineering problem or hardware floor?

Mostly a hardware floor, with one engineering-sized slice. At the bandwidth floor Bolmo still reads 1.23x (bf16) to 1.42x (int8) the bytes of OLMo 2 per generated byte, because its local stack is read once per round (0.33-0.35 rounds per byte) next to the global pass (0.14-0.15 per byte); no kernel can remove those reads on a card whose caches hold 4 MB. The measured byte tax of 1.60x (int8) sits only about 13% above that floor ratio, so a perfect local megakernel could not bring it below ~1.5x; my model predicts 4.3-7.8% of Bolmo's time, which moves the ratio from 1.60x to about 1.53-1.48x. The remaining distance to the floor is large in both models (each runs at ~35-40% of floor speed) and is made of weight-independent work, not of local-stack launch overhead: in the local round it is ~240 tiny kernels (fusable without a megakernel), in the global pass ~2 ms of norms/attention/cache per pass, in the host loop the sync. So the byte tax is a floor in what it reads, and an engineering problem in how long everything else takes; the persistent-megakernel mechanism of the theory (hide barriers and ramps) did not materialise on this card, because a barrier is 1 us and the loss that remains per phase is arrival skew and dependent-chain latency, which prefetching weights cannot hide.

### 7. Deviations, problems and uncertainties

- **The Stage 2 stack is a model of the local round, not the round.** Random weights; simplified mLSTM math; no convolution, no per-head norm, no boundary logic, no embeddings (3 extra phases are added as an allowance of 31 us); M = 1 only. The saving per round for K > 0 rounds is extrapolated (see the model paragraph). Numerical correctness is checked against a torch reference of the same simplified math, not against Bolmo.
- **Variant (a) is not today's real graph.** Task wording: 'today's style: one kernel per phase in a CUDA graph (skinny kernels)'. I used my own 28 fused phase kernels (the best case of that style) and took the real baseline from Stage 0 and the earlier benchmarks (1.347 ms bf16 g8 pair, 0.791 ms int4 pair). The Triton skinny linears were not re-benchmarked inside the chain.
- **Dataflow waits (b) were measured in Stage 1 only.** In the matvec chain every phase needs the whole input vector, so the only head-local dependency is A -> B; I used full barriers in Stage 2. With 1-4 producers saving <= 9% per phase (Stage 1), this cannot change the result.
- **int4 was implemented, not skipped** (group-128 int4 with fp16 scales, tested against the reference) but it is instruction-bound at 23% of copy bandwidth; the int8 and int4 step times in the cost model are therefore an extrapolation by bytes plus the bf16 per-phase loss, as the task allows, and the measured values are shown next to them. A faster dequantizer (packed half2 math, `lop3` tricks) was not tried.
- **Prefetch implementations tried**: end-of-phase `cp.async` staging (80 / 32 KiB), double-buffered staging at the start of the previous phase (80 KiB), mLSTM state preload, first-batch weight loads before the input vector. Not tried: register-level prefetch across the barrier, dynamic row stealing against the arrival skew, `cp.async.bulk` / TMA (not on sm_86), L2 prefetch hints. H62's second part is refuted for these implementations; a better one might exist.
- **Nsight Systems**: the bf16 skinny trace with NVTX ranges could not be imported twice (`AnalysisFailed`/`InvalidArgument`, the int4 trace with the same tool imported fine). I took one trace per K without NVTX (`run_megakernel_stage0c.sh`; the last 30 of 330 identical rounds are analysed). The traced period is ~3% above the untraced benchmark. The int4 K = 0 / K = 4 traces and the bf16 ones are single traces, one prompt state (the graphs are replayed on whatever state is in the buffers).
- **Bandwidth reference.** The task's 411 GB/s was used for floors and percentages; this session's own copy measurements were 393-400 GB/s (read + write counted) and read-only 425 GB/s; GPU clocks drift and were not locked. Step-time differences below ~3% between variants are within noise (the 28-kernel graph and the persistent kernel are the same within it).
- **Bugs found on the way** (all fixed, covered by tests): int4 `xsum` was computed per group of 128 but subtracted per vector of 32 (wrong results); the group-sum loop covered only 8 of 16 groups; the barrier base used 28 instead of 27 barriers per step, so the second launch timed out (caught by the error flag, no hang); mLSTM n / m were read and written by different blocks in the same phase (race) and are now double-buffered by step parity. 10 of 10 injected mutants are killed by `tests/test_megakernel_bench.py` (`tools/mk_mutation.py`, `results/megakernel/mutation.json`); the spin-cap code itself is not mutation-tested because a mutant that ignores the cap would hang the display driver.
- **Overlap.** One CPU-only analysis of the int4 trace (sqlite) ran while the second Nsight trace was being recorded; the full test suite was started only after all measurements. Nothing else used the GPU during a measurement.
- **Tests.** `python -m pytest -q`: 355 passed (310 before this task + 45 in `tests/test_megakernel_bench.py`: reference comparison for the three precisions, persistent / per-phase / graph, two-step state carry, publication check, spin-cap on missing producers in both the sync and the streaming kernel, refusal of non-resident grids, a corrupted-weights check).
- The Colab notebook was not updated; nothing was committed to git.

## Final frontier

Task: `TASK_final_frontier.md`. Hypotheses and predictions copied verbatim before any analysis; backup `results/FINDINGS.before_final_frontier.md`.

### Hypotheses and predictions (pre-registered)

Predictions for the record: A is about 6–8% faster than the reference at
essentially no quality cost; B about 8–12% faster at +1.2% bpb, less than its
bytes suggest because of tinygemm's row growth; the ratio to OLMo 2 int8 drops
to about 1.45–1.5x.

- **H65 (gate):** the cost model predicts at least one of A or B at least 8%
  faster than the reference (1.588 ms/byte, default prompts, 256 new bytes).
- **H66 (measured, if built):** the measured ms/byte of each measured
  configuration is within ±15% of its prediction; A's bpb change is at most
  +0.15%, B's at most +1.5% with a CUTE drop of at most 3 points.
- **H67 (ratio, if built):** in a same-session block, the better of A and B
  divided by OLMo 2 int8 drafted (combined lookup, K = 8, skinny int8 blocks)
  is at most 1.45x.

### Outcome in one paragraph

Stage 0 ran (CPU only, `tools/final_frontier_costmodel.py`, `results/final_frontier/costmodel.{md,json}`). The cost model predicts **A (local int8 + global int8) 6.1% and B (local int4 with M-dispatch + global int8) 7.0% faster** than the reference (global int8 + bf16 local, 1.588 ms/byte), both under the 8% line, so **H65 is refuted, the gate says stop, and nothing was built or measured in Stage 1; H66 and H67 are not tested.** One caveat is stated below and not used to override the gate: a variant of B that is already measured (g8l4, local int4 with the skinny kernel at every row count) runs at 1.430 ms/byte, 9.9% faster than the reference, because its generated text drafts better than the reference's, a text effect the model cannot predict.

### 1. Hypotheses: verdicts

| hypothesis | verdict | numbers |
|---|---|---|
| **H65** gate: the model predicts A or B >= 8% faster than 1.588 ms/byte | **Refuted** (B misses by one point) | A +6.1% (bf16 frame +5.7%, int4 frame +6.6%), B +7.0% (+6.7% / +7.3%); best = B at 1.477 ms/byte |
| **H66** measured within +-15% of prediction; bpb A <= +0.15%, B <= +1.5%, CUTE drop <= 3 | **Not tested** (gate stopped Stage 1) | context only: measured earlier, local int8 bpb +0.08% (torchao-era), g8l4 +1.13% bpb (own int4 format), local int4 CUTE -2.4 (torchao format, 1,500 items) |
| **H67** best of A/B / OLMo 2 int8 drafted <= 1.45x | **Not tested** | model: A 1.50x, B 1.49x (reference 1.60x); only the text-conditional anchored figure of B (1.40x) is below 1.45x |

The stated predictions: *A about 6-8% faster, essentially no quality cost*: model 6.1%, inside the range (unmeasured). *B about 8-12% faster, less than its bytes suggest because of tinygemm's row growth*: model 7.0%, below the range; the row-growth part was right (tinygemm alone, no dispatch, gains only 4.2% by the model, the same as the skinny-only variant's modelled 4.0-4.4%; the dispatch adds about 2.8 points). *Ratio to OLMo 2 int8 about 1.45-1.5x*: model 1.49-1.50x for A and B, at the top of the range (unmeasured).

### 2. Stage 0: predicted local round costs and ms/byte

Method: the cost model of `## Skinny kernels` (`tools/skinny_costmodel.py`), reused unchanged: the measured pieces of the P4 model (verify pair per K, global pass per P) get their linear-kernel time swapped by the kernel tables of `results/skinny/stage{0,1}.json` at the piece's row count; non-matmul kernels (the ~238 small kernels, ~370 us per round) and the gaps stay as measured, so they are the same in every configuration. New: label `int4d` = per shape and row count the faster of tinygemm and the skinny int4 kernel. No timing was missing (all four local shapes have tinygemm and skinny int4 at M = 1..33 in the tables), so no new microbenchmark was run. Because the model cannot predict how quantization changes the generated text (and with it the draft acceptance), it was run in two frames, bf16 pieces with the bf16 P4 tallies and int4 pieces with the int4 P4 tallies; the prediction is the model's ratio to the reference in each frame applied to the measured reference, averaged over the frames. `k_max` / `k_cross` are chosen per configuration on the extended prompt set (as in the earlier tasks), ms/byte are for the default prompts with bf16 activations.

Local linears of one verify round (ms; 5 layers x 8 linears on K + 1 rows plus 2 projections of 1 row), from the kernel tables:

| K (rows) | bf16 skinny | int8 skinny | int4 tinygemm | int4 skinny | int4 dispatch |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 0 (1) | 0.934 | 0.594 | 0.397 | 0.671 | 0.397 |
| 2 (3) | 0.942 | 0.602 | 0.411 | 0.672 | 0.411 |
| 4 (5) | 0.935 | 0.601 | 0.455 | 0.676 | 0.455 |
| 6 (7) | 0.939 | 0.603 | 0.572 | 0.676 | 0.572 |
| 8 (9) | 0.945 | 0.604 | 0.632 | 0.681 | 0.622 |
| 16 (17) | 0.966 | 0.705 | 1.116 | 0.849 | 0.821 |
| 32 (33) | 1.051 | 1.060 | 1.637 | 1.145 | 1.131 |

The skinny int4 kernel beats tinygemm from 8 rows (2048 x 2048), 11 (2048 x 2816) and 12 rows (1024 x 2048, 2816 x 2048). The within-patch operating point (K = 6, 7 rows) is still on tinygemm's side, but its advantage there has shrunk to 0.10 ms of 0.68 (tinygemm adds ~28-45 us per extra row on the big shapes: 0.397 -> 0.572 ms from 1 to 7 rows), and the crossing rounds (K = 24-32) are on the skinny side. int8 skinny is flat in M up to 9 rows (0.594-0.604) and does not lose to bf16 until 33 rows.

Full local rounds (verify pair, i.e. linears + non-matmul + gaps; ms) and the model's P4 predictions, default prompts:

| variant | verify pair K = 0 / 6 | k_cross / k_within (bf16 frame) | ms/byte, bf16 frame | ms/byte, int4 frame | gain vs reference (bf16 / int4 frame) |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---|
| reference: global int8 + local bf16 skinny | 1.358 / 1.511 | 32 / 6 | 1.644 | 1.696 | - |
| **A**: local int8 + global int8 | 1.018 / 1.175 | 24 / 6 | 1.551 | 1.584 | +5.7% / +6.6% |
| B': local int4 skinny + global int8 (= measured g8l4) | 1.096 / 1.248 | 32 / 6 | 1.578 | 1.620 | +4.0% / +4.4% |
| B'': local int4 tinygemm only + global int8 | 0.821 / 1.144 | 24 / 4 | 1.575 | 1.625 | +4.2% / +4.2% |
| **B**: local int4 dispatch + global int8 | 0.821 / 1.144 | 24 / 4 | 1.534 | 1.572 | +6.7% / +7.3% |

(The model reproduces the reference only up to its known bias: 1.644 / 1.696 predicted against 1.588 measured, which is why only ratios are applied.)

Predictions applied to the measured reference of 1.588 ms/byte:

| configuration | predicted ms/byte | gain | predicted ratio to OLMo 2 int8 drafted (0.994, same session as the reference) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| reference | 1.588 (measured) | - | 1.60x (measured) |
| A: local int8 + global int8 | 1.491 | +6.1% | 1.50x |
| B: local int4 dispatch + global int8 | 1.477 | +7.0% | 1.49x |

**Gate (H65): best predicted gain +7.0% (needs >= 8%) -> STOP.** The sources of the shortfall are in the table: the local linears are only part of a local round (A's K = 0 round drops from 1.358 to 1.018 ms, a saving of 0.34 ms at about 0.35 rounds per byte = 0.12 ms/byte = 7.5% of 1.588, minus the cost of larger K rounds at M > 1), and the global pass (~49% of the time) is untouched in both.

**Caveat, reported and not used to override the gate.** The model gives the reference and every variant the same draft acceptance, and the measured cells show that acceptance moves by more than the 1-point margin: g8 got 6.6 bytes per global pass against 7.4 for bf16 P4, and g8l4 got 7.4 again (1.430 ms/byte measured, +9.9% against the reference where the model, same acceptance, said +4.0%). If B' is taken as the anchor (g8l4 measured 1.430 x model(B)/model(B') per frame = 1.390 / 1.387), B would be 1.389 ms/byte, +12.6% against the reference and 1.40x OLMo 2. That number assumes B would inherit g8l4's lucky text; the reference's text is as likely to be the unlucky one (g8 lost 7.8 points to it in the earlier task, bf16-skinny 15 points) as g8l4's to be the lucky one. The pre-registered gate is about the model's prediction, the text-neutral prediction is 7.0%, so I stopped. Anyone who wants the 12.6% cell has a cheaper way to the same neighbourhood: g8l4 is already built and measured (below). What dispatch would add on top of it, 2.8% by the model, would cost a second int4 weight copy and a second quantizer (tinygemm's format against my own), which makes the quantized weights depend on the row count, i.e. drafted and non-drafted would no longer share exactly the same weights (the fp32 exactness check of the earlier tasks assumes one set of weights).

### 3. Measured speed, quality, exactness, ratio

Not run (gate stopped Stage 1). Existing measured cells for the configurations close to A and B are in the table below.

### 4. Final frontier table, Bolmo under P4 (default prompts, 256 new bytes, bf16 activations)

All numbers are copied from the earlier sections; nothing in this table was measured in this task except the two predicted rows. bpb is against Bolmo's own bf16; CUTE is 1,500 items unless noted. The ratio is Bolmo P4 / OLMo 2 drafted (combined lookup K = 8) at the same precision rule (transformer blocks of OLMo 2 at the precision named; embeddings and heads bf16). Floor ratio = both models at the weight-read floor (`## Megakernel theory`, drafted loops); distance = measured ratio / floor ratio.

| configuration | ms/byte | bpb change | CUTE change (points) | ratio to OLMo 2 (same precision) | floor ratio | distance to floor ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| bf16 P4 (cuBLAS) | 1.936 | 0 | 0 | 1.36x (vs 1.421, bf16) | 1.23x | +11% |
| **g8 (reference): global int8 + bf16 local, skinny** | **1.588** | +0.002% | -0.13 | **1.60x** (vs 0.994, int8, same session) | 1.42x | +13% |
| g8l4: global int8 + local int4 (own format, skinny at every M) | 1.430 | +1.13% | not run in the own format; -2.4 with torchao local int4 + global int8 | 1.44x (vs 0.994; different session: B2 against B4) | 1.24x (equal-precision OLMo int8 floor, Bolmo local int4) | +16% |
| all-int4 (tinygemm) | 1.462 | +4.9% (torchao) | -6.2 (500 items) | not measured (no drafted OLMo 2 int4) | not computed for drafted loops (minimal loops 1.44x) | - |
| A (predicted, not built): local int8 + global int8 | 1.491 | ~+0.08% (earlier local int8) | ~+0.2 (500 items) | 1.50x | 1.42x | +5% |
| B (predicted, not built): local int4 dispatch + global int8 | 1.477 | ~+1.2% | ~-2.4 | 1.49x | 1.24x | +20% |

The "+5%" and "+20%" distances of the predicted rows compare a ratio from the model with a floor ratio of different precision settings and are shown for completeness only: A's floor ratio is the int8 one (equal precision), B's is the local-int4 one, and a configuration with an int4 local stack has a lower floor ratio but a larger distance to it.

### 5. Where the project ends

On this card, Bolmo 1B with the best near-lossless stack (global int8, bf16 local, P4 gated lookup drafting, skinny kernels) generates a byte in 1.588 ms against 0.994 ms for OLMo 2 1B with int8 blocks and lookup drafting: a byte tax of 1.60x, which is 13% above the 1.42x that two models at the weight-read floor would show, because the byte-level model reads its local stack once per round (0.35 rounds per byte) and the global stack once per 6.6 bytes. The two ways of buying more were priced here: moving the local stack to int8 (A) saves 6% by the model at no quality cost, to int4 (B) 7% at +1.2% bpb and about -2 CUTE points, and the measured int4 cell that already exists (1.430 ms/byte, 1.44x) is the practical upper end of what weights alone can do, for a quality cost. Neither reaches the 1.4x region without the ~2 ms of non-matmul work per global pass (about a third of the global-pass time and ~0.3 ms/byte) and the ~240 small kernels per local round, which no weight format touches. The remaining byte tax on this card is therefore about 1.4-1.6x per byte at the same precision, of which the part set by what must be read (1.23-1.42x) cannot be removed, and the rest (13-16%) is fusion work that the earlier tasks priced at a few percent each. This is the end of the optimization line; the project moves to the write-up.

### 6. Deviations, limits, tests

- **Gate decision is the strict reading.** The model-only prediction of B (+7.0%) misses the gate by one point; I did not use the text-anchored +12.6% to pass it (reasons above). A different reading would have built the M-dispatch (the only new piece), measured A and B with quality and exactness runs, and a ratio block. The cost of that was not spent; the user can overrule the gate.
- **Two frames.** The model has no acceptance model for quantized text; the two tally frames (bf16, int4) bracket it. Their predictions differ by 0.6-0.9 points per configuration.
- **Table interpolation.** Row counts between the benchmarked M values (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 8, 9, 16, 17, 33) are interpolated linearly; tinygemm at the K = 6 operating point (7 rows) is thus an interpolation between 5 and 9 rows. Kernel microbenchmarks are from the skinny task's session; the local K = 0 and K = 6 rounds of A and B were not measured end to end.
- **Not covered by the model:** the 16-row gate projections (untouched in the skinny task as well), an int8 or int4 `lm_head`, extra launches that a dispatch layer would add, mixed-quantizer text effects.
- **Tests:** only a CPU tool script was added (`tools/final_frontier_costmodel.py`); no switch, kernel or default path changed, so no default-path test was extended. `python -m pytest -q`: 355 passed in 203 s (`results/final_frontier/pytest.log`). The GPU was idle during Stage 0 (no measurement was taken). Nothing was committed to git. The Colab notebook was not updated.

Artifacts: `tools/final_frontier_costmodel.py`, `results/final_frontier/costmodel.{md,json}`, `results/final_frontier/pytest.log`. Backup of FINDINGS.md before this task: `results/FINDINGS.before_final_frontier.md`.
